THE AMBULANCE BRIGADE.

SATURDAY'S REVIEW AND COMPETI- TIONS AT WARRINGTON.

Warrington is fortunate in possessing an enthusias- tic band of ambulance men whose interest and profici- ency in the praiseworthy undertaking of rendering "first aid' ta the injured show no signs of diminishing. On the contrary the Corps has been making rapid strides of late, and the seal of the members combined with a capable management augur well for the future success of the movement in this town. It was cer- tainly an honour to the Corps that Warrington should be selected as the meeting place of some hundreds of ambulance workers who journeyed here on Saturday to take part in the twelfth annual review and competitions in connection with the North-East Lancashire corps and divisions of the Brigade. Numerous towns were represented the event being one of unusual interest and importance to all members of the Brigade in the district. The morning was devoted to squad competi- tions, the men being put through various tests at the police station and the nursing sisters at the gymnasium. The judges were :—Division 1, Sargeonj Captain F. A. Brooks, Felixstowe, Superintendent Secretary, number three district, and Mr. T. H. Woolston, Assistant Com- missioner number three district. Division 2, Mr. W. E. Audland, Wellingborough, chief surgeon number three district, and Superintendent Harvey Reeves, Northamp- ton corps; police squads, Lieut. W. A. Gibb, M.D., Ipswich, and Surgeon Lieutenant Colonel E. Cureton," V.D„ Shrewsbury; nursing squads, Dr. S. 0. Eades Ipswich, and Surgeon Major Sleman. The tests con- sisted of tasks set by the judges in rendering "first aid," questions, practical work &c„ and the following squads competed :—Division 1, Kendal, Accrington, Brierfield, Blackpool, and Preston; division 2, Whalley, Barrow- ford, Liverpool, Southport, Warrington, Morecambe and Oldham; nursing squads, Accrington,Barrowford, Brier- field, Blackpool and Preston ; police squads, Manchester City, Bootle, Salford, Warrington, Preston, and Black- pool. The event of the day was the review on the Rugby Football Field. The various corps, divisions and police corps assembled on the lawn in front of the Town Hall at 2.45 in the afternoon and marched in pro- cession to the Football field headed by Messrs. Walker's Brewery band. The weather was beautifully fine, and many spectators assembled along the line of route. The ambulance men looked very smart in their well- kept uniforms and marched in fine military style. The Warrington police horse ambulance brought up the rear of the procession and made a capital turn out. When the processionists arrived at the field the nursing sisters had already been formed into line in readiness for the review. The attendance of the public was disappointingly small in fact, the popular side was almost unoccupied The inspecting officer was Lieutenant Colonel C. J. Trimble, C.M.G., V.D., deputy commissioner of the district, who has previously visited Warrington to inspect the local corps. The number of ambulance men on parade was 304 with 35 stretchers, 1 litter, and 1 horse ambulance, and there were also 156 nurses and 33 policemen. The Inspector having passed through the ranks, the march-past took place, headed by the nurses. The sight was a very interesting one and both men and nurses marched so well as to gain the cordial praise of therinspecting officer. Several exercises were afterwards gene through with precision and there were practical demonstrations of the importance of efficient "first aid." The men proved themselves to be exceedingly well trained and attended to a variety of" injuries"with marked promptitude and skill. It should be mentioned that:in addition to the towns represented by the squads examined in the morning teams were on parade from Abram Colliwy and Burnley.

===== 0002601_19030626_art0008.txt | included=true | included =====
SPEECH BY THE MAYOR.

Before the announcement of the results the MAYOR (Mr. J. Charlton Parr) delivered a short speech. He said it was his proud privilege in the name of Warring- ton to welcome all the ambulance men and nursing sisters, and it was his privilege as president of the local Association to say how glad he and the Mayoress were to see such a largc gathering of members of the Brigade. He might say that during the war now so happily con- cluded the brigade was recognized for the first time as part and parcel of our imperial forces. The members had rendered good service in South Africaland they all knew that the men would be quite ready to render good services again in ease those services should unfortun- ately—because war was always a misfortune—be called into requisition. Though they had not had to light material forces of flesh and blood, they had had to fight the great enemies of humanity—pain and death—and in many instances they had been able to conquer pain, and to resist and drive away death. What more noble cause could any man fight in? He was pleased to learn that out of 2,000 ambulance men sent to assist our soldiers when help was most needed, 900 came from the northern district, and a very good proportion—he believed 27— came from our good old town of Warrington (Applause.) He was proud, as representing Warrington, to feel that the spirit of patriotism was alive, and that the ambulance men were ready to do their utmost, and to be at the front when their services w.re required. He was sure that meetings of that kind would encourage the general public to take an interest in the movement and support It. They were happy to feel that the King was at the head of the Order of St. John of Jerusalem, which was the great iorder for rendering assistance in all the accidents to which our poor human nature was most easily amenable. They all recognized the King's good qualities and they knew that his Majesty and the Queen were most anxious to assist in every way in promoting the welfare of their subjects. (Applause.) THE INSPECTING OFFICER ANNOUNCES THE

RESULTS.

Lieutenant-Colonel TRIMBLE said it had been a very pleasant duty to carry out the inspection. That was their twelfth annual review and when he threw his mind back to their .first attempt at an inspection and review he looked with very great pleasure on the parade that afternoon. They now appeared to be if not a military at all events a semi-military organization. Their corps and divisions were well drilled and well-uniformed and the officers appeared to have acquired a substantial knowledge of their drill. The men seemed to take an interest in the work and had turned out in a smart and soldier-like fashion. He was very much impressed with the appearance of the uniforms which were on the whole well-fitting and clean. The officers were particularly smartly dressed and !,et a fine example to the men. The parade of the nursing sisters was a very strong feature indeed. His colleagues who came from the South of England told him they wondered how the corps in the north could work so much good among the nursing sisters. He did not know what kind of nursing sisters they had in the south, but he might say. that the nursing sisters in this district were most amenable to their deputy commis- sioner and other officers. (Laughter.) These girls were constantly doing an unlimited amount of good, but as much of it was done in their homes it was not known to the public at large. He would like to draw the attention of the men to the important fact that it had been lately established that members of the brigade could volunteer for service in the Royal navy in case of national necessity. The matter had not been taken up as keenly in the district as was usually the case. He wanted the officers to im- press upon the minds of the men the necessity of the district, doing its duty as far as the Royal Navy Sick Reserve was concerned. The men who piped simply gave their word that they would' serve in a naval hospital or on board a man-of-war or cruiser in case of necessity. They had to come forward for inspection once a year and prove themselves efficient as "first- ' aiders." Lieutenant-Colonel Trimble then commented

IS NIGHTMARE EVER FATAL? '

"Rarely," said a physician to whom the question was put the other day. The terrible visions which fall under this description are generally due to disturbances of the digestion. During sleep parts of the body a.e being replenished, and food should be taken before ileeping in order to supply the nutriment for this. When it causes night- mare, some corrective should be applied. Ono or two bile beans form an excellent corrective, as they are purely vegetable in nature and operate differectly to the old-fashioned and imperfect medicines of the fifty years in use' type." This valuable advice from such a well informed quarter is confirmed by practice. "I had liver trouble which so disordered my digestion as to some times make me absolutely unable to retain food. At night I often could not sleep at all, and when I did sleep I had most awful nightmare. I always used to dream of death !" So says Miss Mabel Mills, whose address is given below. "Often when moving about the house was so dizzy that I would for a few minutes be coot pletely dazed. My headaches were most acute, and many a day I have gone about with vinegar bandages round my head. Constipation was also present and caused me considerable pain. Until I tried Bile Beans I sought in vain for relief. This vegetable .prepara- tion, however, proved equal to my difficult case, and from beginning with it I rapidly improved. lam now cured, my digestion is sound, and I can sleep with- out being troubled by disquieting dreams." Miss Mills livesat "The Woodlands,' West Green, Tottenham. The operation of bile beans is particularly mild. They do not merely purge, giving temporary relief only, like the old-fashioned so-called remedies of forty or fifty years ago. They act directly on the liver and digestive organs, strengthening and stimulating those organs to perform their natural duties. They can be discontinued after the cure is effected; they are purely vegetable; they contain no harmful drugs, and they are the safest medicine. Do not accept any other preparation offered as "just as good" and with a similar sounding name.

===== 0002601_19030626_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
MARRIAGE, SEPARATION, AND SUICIDE IN

EIGHT WEEKS,

Quite a sensation was caused in Hayfield at a late hour on Tuesday evening, when, after pluckily dragging the Kinder printworks reservoir alone for four hours, Police-constable Hill recovesed the body of Robert Tonge (23), a quarryman well-known in the place. Tonge had only been married eight weeks' and for the past month had been in ill-health. On Monday the young couple separated, the wife going to New Mills and the husband to his father's in Hayfield. At three o'clock in the morning Tonge left his bed and did not return; his boots were found in an outbuilding, and later in the day his cap was found floating on the water of the reservoir, from which the officer subsequently recovered the body.

BY HARRIS BURLAND, AUTHOR OF ibACOBRA", ETC.

CHAPTER IV. rue HOUSE IN THE WOODS.

THE little village of Garth lies on the shores of Cardigan Bay. It has but a single stmet of small white houses extending for nearly a mile along the Coast. The backs of those nearest the sea are built up and fortified with row after row of piles and balks of timber fastened together with iron chains and clamps. For the sea is fierce when the tides are high and a west wind is blowing in Cardigan Bay. The very houses themselves are low built to meet the storms, and the plates are strength- ened with layers of cement. But year after year the sea takes its toll of the inhabitants. It sweeps their homes with water and strikes them with flying shingle. It fills the back yards knee deep with foaming waves and carries away the outhouses. At one end of the village it even undermines the masonry itself, and beats some hapless cottage into a heap of bricks and mortar. And year after year the patient folk rebuild, and re-fortify, and replace, content to live on their ridge by the courtesy of an angry ocean, though within half a mile the cliffs begin to ,rise from the beach, and their houses might be built on solid rock for all time.

The inhabitants of this little place are chiefly sailors and fisherfolk. Captains are exceeding plentiful. Some have retired on their savings and make a rich harvest by yating out *their tiny rooms at exorbitant ates to visitors in the summer. Others are on active service. For months, for a year, for two years perhaps they disappear and return with considerable savings and much to tell to those that I wait for them. Rough fellows some of them are, mostly small and tough, but men to whom the nation owes much. they live a hard life and enjoy their rest kvhen it comes to them. A stranger walking down the street would say he had never seen so many loafers in a village before—strong able bodied fellows lounging in the doorways with pipes in their mouths and absolutely idle from morning to night. Yet more than one of them has compressed as much physical labour and mental anxiety into forty-eight hours as most people experience in six months.

To the north of the village the country lies flat and low, a mere line of sand hills and shingle ending in the estuary of the Llyn. To the south it rises into hills, and the coast line changes to cliffs 500 or Goo feet in height. with enormous rocks piled up at their bases, through which the sea rushes with thunderous clamour, and the 'burst of foam and spray. Directly behind the village and almost on a level with the high tides stretches the great bog of Gogerddan. It is intersected in every direction with dykes, and a small river flows thrOugh it from the hills to join the Llyn. Some of these dykes are filled with dark brown water and are absolutely stagnant. Others feel the ebb and the flow of the tides, and their depth depends on the movements of the sea up the estuary some four miles off. The bog is covered with heather and rushes and is almost valueless as pasture. The peat, however, supplies twenty villages with fuel, and the stacks of it break ;the monotony of the surface with tall brown heaps, built •up square and sturdy like the towers of some old fortalice.

All round this bog and on the far side of the estuary rise the mountains, towering out of woods into barren escarpments of rock. Most of them are •over two thousand feet in height, and they form so gigantic and impos- ing a wall that one is surprised to ;3 e e further heights rising above them in the far distance. These are part of the great barrier of Wales, and to this day they have kept the Welsh customs and Welsh language as living things in spite of the great tide of Saxons that beat against them from the East.. At .the foot of these hills lies the village of Trethol, noted through all the country side for its flannels, which are woven on hand- looms in the houses of the ccfttagers. And looking across the bog from Garth one large house stands out white and clear among the trees, and when the sun is setting its long line of windows turn it into a bar of flame. Behind it and on either side the woods run up the mountain. In front its gardens slope down to a long grey wall, beneath which the woods again encircle it, and the tops of some of the trees are almost on a level with the coping of the wall, and so steep is the descent that it is almost possible to lean over and touch their boughs.

Here, one evening in May, nearly twelve months after the events recorded it;‘, the last chapter, a young girl stood alone and gazed across the Gogerddan to the sea. In the light of the setting sun something sparkled on her cheek like a drop of liquid gold. Her face was very beautiful, and her beauty was only emphasised by the sorrow in her eyes as she gazed out across the sea. In her hand she held a small scrap Of paper, ragged and creased with much handling. Every now and then she would read it and press it to her lips. It only contained four verses of indifferent pooetry ; but even Shakespeare might have been proud of such a tribute from so fair a maid. The lines ran as follows:

Have you remembrance of that night. The long low line of tawny sand, The sullen stretch of marshy land Beneath the mountains' purple height The cottage lights upon the lea, The ebon cliffs against the sky, The weary curlew's farewell cry From shadowed field to silent sea;

The path ascending round the hill, The blurred expanse of grey below, The fenpools flaming with the glow Of one gold bar that lingered still ;

Then dark against the topaz light, Two lonely figures, man and maid, And one too trembling and afraid, Have you remembrance of that night?

They were signed " .E.T." "Have I remembrance ? " she murmured to herself. "Have I remembrance ? " And then the sun sank below the horizon, and the one gold bar lingered still, and she heard the cry of the curlew, and saw the flaming light on the fenpools. It was just 4ich a scene as the verses described. " night" came back to her, and she burst into tears. She was roused from her mournful reverie by a:harsh vOice calling from the lawn : Mavanwy r Mavanwy 1" "Yes, father." _ .

"Do you know the time ? Dinner is in half an hour, and you haven't started to dress .yet. You know .the Walroyds are coming to-night? " "Yes, father; I am coming." She hastily dried her eyes and turned her back on the West. But before she -entered the house she looked back once more, and saw the sails of a brigantine silhouetted black against the golden sky. She bit her lip, and passed in. The very F' ';ht of a ship was agony to her. She put on a plain black dinner-frock and came down to the drawing-rooin, There she found her father standing by the fireplace. There was a look of impatience on his face, and he held a watch in his hand.

"It is past the dinner-hour," he said, abruptly. .1 When I was young, guests had more courCesy. and the hostess was always ready to receive them half an hJur before they were due."

Mavanwy smiled sadly. " They are Americans, father," she said gently. " Their ideas are probably dil ferent."

Air. ,lorgan looked at her with disapproval. "Is that your best dress ? " he said, harshly. !' You look as if you were voing tu a funeraL4

" Black suits me, father," she answered; and she looked at herself in the glass with a little smile of vanity..

And indeed she looked superb, with het ,irk hair and dark eyes, and her white arms and shoulders flashing from the sombre material of her dress. ''Yet it was not for that reason she wore black, and her father knew it, and it was the knowledge that made him speak so bitterly. As a rule, he cared nothing for a woman's dress, and regarded any smart- ness as a sign of ethpty vanity. "You are not old , enough to wear black," he answered.

Then he consulted his 'watch again, and frowned.

A hard, cold man was John Morgan of Llynglas. Bred up in a strict Nonconformist family, he added all his natural harshness of character to the somewhat stern and narrow principles of his particular sect. He was poor, but poverty had had no chastening effect upon his mind. It had only served to warp his soul and arouse the worst traits of his nature. In his father's lifetime he had seen a fine property dwindle down to a few hun- dred acres of barren land, and a house which was only a mockery when there was no money to keep it up. He had not even the satis- faction of knowing that this was due to extrava- gance, and that he could lay the blame on a dead man. It had been due solely to a grasp- ing spirit which he had himself inherited, a desire to make more money without the ability to make it, a longing for io per cent. instead of a safe 4 per cent., an inclination to enter into the wildest schemes if they held out promise of immense fortunes. It is often the peculiarity of the hard and grasping man, that though he will save pennies at every turn, and strive his utmost to do other men out of odd shillings, yet he is utterlyl reckless when his vision is dazzled by prospects of gold and he is the easiest prey of the clever and unscrupulous adventurer. Such a man had been Morgan Morgan, and his son now reaped the fruits of his avarice. The great house was almost as empty as a barn. The finest pictures had gone from the walls, and had not even been replaced by inferior copies. The library was a mere row of empty bookselves. The dining-room would have disgraced a third-rate restaurant. The grounds about the house were ill-kept and overgrown with weeds. But the sight of all these things failed to impress John Morgan with the vanity of earthly possessions. It only filled him with a strong and immovable purpose, to make good the waste, to restore the fortunes of his family at any cost.

It was therefore the irony of fate that two years ago young Emrys Tredegar should have fallen in love with his daughter and won her heart. The young man, though of a family even superior to his own, was, if possible, in a still more hopeless financial condition. But Emrys Tredegar was dead—or, to use a phrase made familiar by the South African war, " missing "—and it seemed now that matters might work out to a satisfactory conclusion. Before young Tredegar had sailed for Peru, he had let his house to two rich American brothers—the Walroyds, of Chicago. They were .reputed to be millionaires, and though nothing was known of theiri previous history, that was . nothing to their discredit. They hailed from a country where men became rich in a single night, and no one comments :on the fact. The elder brother Cyrus P. Wal- royd was undoubtedly in love with Mavanwy. He had even expressed his desire to marry an Englishwoman of good family and re- -suseitate the fortuneof some fallen house for his own children.. The only obstacle to the consummation of this ideal match was Mavanwy herself. And that is why 'on this night her father snapped like an angry dog at her, and the mere sight of her black dress irritat- ed him beyond endurance. He looked once more •at his watch, and tapped his feet impatiently. Mavanwy had seated herself in a chair, and was idly turn- ing over the pages of a magazine. His mood was such that he could not leave her , alone.

"I hope you will be civil to these VValroyds to-night, May.anwy," he said, abruptly. "They are people I wish to keep friends with." •

" Why should I not be civil ? " she an- swered, scarcely raising her eyes from her book.

"The last time they were here you were barely so. But I must ask you to remesober that my •guests must be treated with cor- diality."; _

Mavanwy flushed, and looked her father straight in the face. "Which one do you wish mc to be most civil—to be most cordial to ? "

Mr. Morgan bit his lips with vexation and turned his back on his daughter. Then he suddenly. swung round and faced .her.

"Cyrus Walroyd loves you,. Mavanwy," he said sternly. "I am honoured" he said coldly, "and I am sorry for him. I am, as you know, not likely to return his affection." At that moment there was a loud ring at the bell, and a heavy knock at the hall door. A few moments later the door was flung open by a wizened old butler attired in very ill- fitting clothes, and two men entered. May- anwy rose to meet them and extended her hand with a smile. The elder took it in silence, and the marks of his muscular fingers could be seen on the flesh when he loosened it from his grasp. .y.The younger took it with ,more elaborate courtesy and less warmth. Both apologised curtly, for their unpunctuality which was due to the breaking of a shaft on Taliesin Hill. They had walked the last three quarters of a mile. The two brothers were fine men, lean, square built, • and tall. John T. Walroyd, the younger, had the high cheek bones and keen angular face which in some mysterious way the true born American has inherited from the soil, as the North American Indian did before him. The features of the elder were of a coarser type. The face was mote square and the jaw more heavy. It was plain that both were masterful men, each in his own way, the,. elder by brute force, and the younger by sheer tenacity of purpose which would travel by many a devious path, but always keep the goal in sight. They would both have been handsome if their clean shaven faces had not been so deeply lined. To a casual observer they would have seemed types of the strenuous business man, whose fortune has been built up by sheer hard' work, and has cost many an anxious clay and sleepless night to prevent it from tottering to the ground. But a student of character would have .seen an expression in their eyes which had nothing to do with the making and retaining of money. In one the savage nature of a tiger, in the other the keen look of a wolf that is hunting down its prey, and yet has itself to avoid the hunter.

Both men were faultlessly attired. Their coats were just the right length, their trousers just the right cut. They had not an article of jewelry between them, and even their studs were of plain white enamel. Their ties hit the happy medium between severe sim- plicity and artistic negligence. To all appear- ance they . were two ordinary English gentle- men of tact and culture. Only a keen obser- ver could have detected something out of the common behind their mark of politeness, something that spoke vaguely of another life they haZlived, a life in which evening clothes and faultless white ties were unknown quantities. The dinner was, as such things go, a success. It is true that the food was plain, and that the cook had done nothing to elaborate the scanty materials at her command. It was also true that the wine was indifferent. But both host mitt hostess proved themselves accomplished entertainers. Mavanwv's natural sweetness

of character showed itself to the best advan- tage, though her heart was sad, and she dis- liked at least one of her guests. Her father in spite of his harsh nature, was a man of no ordinary ability, and whatever he had to say about a subject was worth listening to. The two Walroyds were full of anecdote, There beemed to be few parts of the world that they had not seen, and their terse quiet method of narrating the most stirring incidents would have been a valuable ol*ct lesson to many novelists. After dinner Mavanwy left the men to smoke their cigars, and strolled out on the terrace. It was a warm night and she longed for the fresh air. The whole atmosphere of the dining-room had been false and artificial. She knew well that at least three members of the party had been thinking of one thing and talking about another. That Cyrus Walroyd had, been thinking of her, and had scarcely been able to conceal the passion in his eyes. That her father had been thinking of restored prosperity; that she herself had been thinking of Emrys Tredegar. And though she did not know it, John Walroyd's mind had been farther atvay than any of the other three. The moon had risen, and the bog of Gogerd- dan was . white with wreaths of mist. All round it the mountains towered up like a gigantic wall of ebony. In the distance the little village of Garth showed like a dim black line. The sea was marked with a broad path of silver. A single ship stood out against the glistening waves. It was motionless and apparently riding at anchor. Mavanwy could see its lights and she sighed. There was happiness in one cottage to-night. She knew that whenever a ship anchored in the bay it meant the brief but joyful visit of some father, or brother, or husband. In the morn- ing he would put off again, and the ship would go its way. A faint breeze came from the sea and stirred the tops of the pines beneath her feet. Then she saw a tiny speck of bYack moving towards the vessel. It was a boat. She turned away and buried her face in her hands.

A few minutes afterwards she heard foot- steps and saw the red tip of a cigar glowing through the darkness. Then a white shirt front gleamed through the shrubs, and Cyrus Walroyd came to her side. "It is better here than indoors, Miss Morgan," he .said abruptly. "My brother and your father are talking business. I looked for you in the drawing room." "It is a lovely night," she answered simply, "but it is getting chilly," and she half turned as though to go. "Yes, it is a lovely night," he said, throw- ing his cigar to the ground, and coming a little closer to her so as to bar her path. She looked up at him and smiled a little uneasily. She knew what was coming, and thought it best to get it over. She sat down on the coping of the wall, and looked out towards the sea.

"The moonlight on the waters is beauti- ful," she said after a pause. "One can scarcely imagine that ship to be real. -It ought to have silver masts and sails of cloth of gold, and be laden with jewels, an.l apes, and peacocks. Its crew should be swarthy men of the East with rings in the r ears and swords and gay sashes. But I fe...r they are very ordinary people and that the cargo is most likely coal." "Do you not like coal, Miss Morgan," he said, "and—and ordinary people? To me they are more useful than apes and peacocks, and dark eyed Easterns, and being more useful, are better."

"I think," she replied, "that one wants something useless and picturesque now-a- days. There is too much talk of facts, and progress, and machinery. Life is not all a commercial undertaking." "Nor is it all a dream," he answered tenderly, "though of late even I have been dreaming. Miss Morgan, I am a plain man, who has roughed it in all parts of the world. I have had to fight my way and I have got many scars in the process. I am rich, but there is something I want more than wealth. I have not the pretty language of the poets at my command, but I will tell you what I want in half a dozen words: Mavanwy, I love you, and I want you to be my wife." He came closer towards her, and tried to take her hand. She drew it gently aside and stared out at the silver pathway on the sea, as though she expected to see someone rise out of the waves and come towards her. For a minute or two there was silence. A solitary curlew cried down in the bog of Gogerddan, and in the far distance there was the sound of a horse's hoofs clattering along the road to Trethol. The man's eyes watched her face hungrily, but the face was almost like a mask of stone.

" Mavanwy," he repeated, "I have asked you •to be my wife. I am rich, and your marriage with me would meet .with your father's approval. I love you passionately. This is not the love of a boy. It is something too strong and terrible to speak much about. I cannot pour words of love into your ears. If I spoke them I do not know what I might say,"

-She turned round and looked at his face. The moonlight was upon it, and the dark lines looked as though they had been traced with a pencil on the face of a marble statue. It was grim, drawn, and immovable. Only the deepset eyes glowed with passion, and they almost seemed to devour her as she met them with her own quiet gaze. "It is impossible, Mr. Walroyd," ' she answered in a low voice. "Do you hot know it is impossible ? " " Emrys Tredegar is dead," he answered curtly. "His ship never reached Lima. It was due there more than a year ago. One of the boats was found floating bottom upwards in the South Pacific. Another ship reported the burnt hull of a vessel 200 miles east of where the boat was found. There is little doubt that it was the "White Swallow." All this is history, known to everyone. lam not

inventing it for my own purposes." Again there was silence. The curlew had ceased to cry and had gone to rest. It was more than a minute before Mavanwy answered. She looked out again at the sea with parted lips and clasped hands. Then she got off the wall and faced the man. "Even if he is dead," she said in a low strained voice, "it does not matter. Before he left England I swore I would be true to him and wait for his return. If he comes I am still here to receive him. If he never returns, I have but to wait until I die. Women have done that before now, Mr. Walroyd. They are not so weak as men suppose." The man's eyes blazed fiercely, and striding towards the girl he caught her by the wrists and looked into her eyes. The brute • beast had risen within him, and he could no longer contain himself.

"It matters not whether he be living or dead," he said hoarsely; "I love ,you, and you shall be my wife. I can wait; I have rarely waited in vain for anything that I desire. Do you know that the very house you live in is mine; that this land is mine.if I choose to take it, that I have bought your father body and soul, and could turn you both out of here to morrow." "In other words," she said with a con- temptuous curl of her upper lip, "you have tried to buy me. Let go of my hands, Mr. Walroyd. Your touch is loathsome to me. There are other houses to live in than this. As for my father, he can make his own bar- gains and pay his own debts. A few minutes ago I respected you, though I could not marry you. Masterful men like you compel respect, but one does not respect wild animals. Let go my hands, I say, or I will call out." Her face was white with fury, and for the moment she felt more like a trapped tigress than a sweet natured woman. Her whole soul had risen in revolt at this outrage. Her mind, filled with loving thoughts of the dead, was stung to madness by the rude grasp of this man. Her eyes flashed dangerously. She would have struck him in the face if her hands had been free.

Cyrus Walroyd laughed. This was a: woman after his own heart, and he loved her all the more for this outbreak• of passicn. But a second later a look of horror crossed his face, his jaw fell, and suddenly loosing her wrists he struck savagely at something that was running across one of his own hands. It was only a little harmless spider. But he had struck, as though it had been an adders

Nothing remained of it but a small blotch of blood. • He rubbed his hand vigorcy with his handkerchief, and removed all traces of it. Then he looked up quickly to see if Mavanwy had noticed his action. But she was several yards away from him. He hesitated for a moment, and then turning sharply he walked up the path, and re-entered the house. (To be continued.)

THE

THE FREE CHURCHES.

CAPTURING THR BOARD SCHOOLS.

A great deal of interest has been aroused by the publication, week by week in the Daily News of facts in connection with the adminis- tration of the Act in different parts of the country. These articles are being contributed by a special commissioner of the National Free Church Council, and are based on the returns furnished by Free Church Council secretaries all over the country. The Education Com- mittee of the National Free Church Council prepared schedules dealing with such points as the composition of local Education Autho- rities, and local Education Committees, denominational and political connections of members of these bodies, &c. A great deal of valuable information has been secured by this means, and one fact is being made more and more prominent, viz., that in many parts of the country the clerical party are capturing the nation's schools. Under the management of the School Board issues between the party which favours "definite religious teaching" and that which desires to maintain the compromise of 1870 could be fought out and representatives chosen who would express the people's wishes in this matter. Now, however, that the management of the schools has been transferred to a body elected to pave the people's streets and mend the drains, and with the additional weapon of co-option, the issue is no longer direct, and grave injustice has been done to the rights of Free Churchmen.

TWO UNITARIANS REPRESENT FREE CHURCHE S.

A glaring instance of the denied representa- tion of Free Churchmen is cited from the Parliamentary division of West Dorset, where the Free Churches are represented on the Education Committee by two Unitarians (one a lady). The headmasters of these schools are all Anglicans; the managers of four of them have fifteen Anglicans, including four clergy- men and a Roman Catholic among them. Another striking example is furnished from Lewes. Here the local Education Authority is the Town Council, and of its twentv-two members no fewer than nineteen are Anglicans, while three only are Free Churchmen. On the Education Committee, twelve members have been appointed from the Town Council—nine Anglicans and three Free Churchmen. Six were co-opted—three Anglicans (two clergy- men and one lady), one Roman Catholic (a priest), and two Free Churchmen (one of whom is a minister). So that on the whole Educa- tion Committee five members only represent the Free Churches.

===== 0002601_19030626_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HOUSE OF CECIL IN EDUCATION.

Politicians have long enjoyed the amicable family arrangement, whereby the principal offices of government in this country have been placed at the disposal of one family. Why should this be confined to politics, and why, if the Premier places his nephew in power and his other relatives in office, should not a school- mistress be represented on the Board of Management of her own school? Such a ques- tion might well be asked of the instance brought to light in connection with a village school in Huntingdonshire. There is but one school in the village to which all the children go, and this is presided over by a headmistress. At the election of foundation-managers the vicar took the chair. and when a certain nomination was

--ado iar.the position of manager, he refused 114; put it to the meeting as the gentleman was a Nonconformist. Ultimately the husband of the mistress was appointed, also the husband's father. It needs little imagination to conjure up the state of bliss in which the management of this school can be conducted—at the public expense.

===== 0002601_19030626_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKING THE ACT TO KILL THE ACT.

Mr. Lloyd George's scheme to work the Act to kill the Act has assumed practical form. The National Free Church Council has just issued in pamphlet form the speech delivered before the Bristol Free Church Council on "The Work- ing of the Education Act." Mr. Lloyd George strongly expresses his view that the Act should be worked so thoroughly that Church- men would join the Passive Resistance move- ment, and call meetings to secure the repeal of the Act. He details the means his own Councils are adopting in this connection, and puts his view in a clear and practical manner.

OPEN-AIR EVANGELISM.

Although the occasional unseasonableness of the weather has at times led people to forget the delights of the meadow and sea-shore, yet the calendar reminds us that we are starting one of the chief holiday months. Acting on the method of going to the crowds, the Free Church Councils all over the country are arranging open-air services on Sundays and week-clays. The Free Church Chronicle for July presents examples of what is being done for the evange- lisation of masses at seaside resorts. The National Free Church Council has once more brought its Mission Van into operation, and this is at present situated at Cambridge, with the Rev. Jesse Mayo in charge.

THE REV. R. J. CAMPBELL IN AMERICA.

The Rev. R. J. Campbell, the well-known minister of the City Temple, London, is now undergoing the fatigues of an American tour. Extensive arrangements have been made, and he has already numerous preaching and other engagements, so that his visit will by no means be a holiday. A thmit,Thtful sermon by Mr. Campbell appears in this month's Free Church- man, and is entitled "The Near Vision of Jesus." A splendid portrait accompanies it.

===== 0002601_19030626_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
A BEAUTIFUL HOME FOR GIRLS.

In spite of the Education struggle, the work of the Free Church Girls' Guild is going on apace. In many parts of the country, homes are being acquired where girls can meet, and where social gatherings can be held. The Guild at Sheffield has just decided to acquire a beau- tiful house in Broomhall-park as a permanent home. It will be not only a residential home for a few girls whose work in Sheffield has necessitated their leaving home, but also a social centre for, it is hoped, a large number of young women employed in shops and business houses, schools, &c., and who by family and upbringing have Nonconformist as- sociations. It is intended to put the management in the hands of a com- petent educated lady superintendent. About kl5O is to be spent on furnishing, and a sum of £.250 a year will be needed to carry it on, at any rate at first, though it is hoped that the house will become self-supporting as far as the boarders are concerned. Accommodation will be provided for ten or a dozen resident mem- bers; the rest of the house will be furnished for the social purposes of the general membership of the Guild.

FREE CHURCH FEDERATION ON THE CONTINENT

The Rev. Thomas Law, the secretary of the National Free Church Council, has arranged to visit Paris for the purpose of spreading the Federation movement, and has also promised to visit Rome, where a large Evangelical Fre* Church Council is already in active ope,atima.

CARDIRtd. VAUGHONS UPC.

The main facts in Cardinal Vaughan's career can be briefly "told. Atter being a pupil fast of the Jesuits at Stonyhurst and then of the Benedictines at Downside, he went, in about his twentieth year, to the Academia Ecclesi- astica at Rome to be trained for the priest- hood. There he -met Manning, destined to be his predecessor as Cardinal and Archbishop,who had become a • Catholic no t long before, and had gone to Rome in -the autumn of 1 8 51. The two formed an inti- mate friendship, which lasted till Manning's death -though it was, perhaps, weak- ened by the divergence of opinion between them during the last years of Manning's life, when some of his views underwent a great change. Soon after his return to England as a priest, Father Vaughan was made vice - president of St. Edmund's College, Old Hall, and he subse- quently joined the community of the Oblates of St. Charles Borrameo at Bayswater, of which Manning was the founder and first head. In I 872 Manning's influence obtained for his disciple the bishopric of Salford, and twenty years later, when Manning's death voided the Archbishopric of Westminster, the 4Present Pope chose Bishop Vaughan to be the great car- dinal's successor. Cardinal Vaughan was all his life an ardent adherent of the Ultramon- tane party in the Roman Catholic Church. As Archbishop of Westminster, however, he was hardly a success; he was well-meaning, but tactless, and (as his friend, Dr. W. G. Ward, admitted) quite unintellectual. The mistakes he made were all the more glaring when . they were compared, as they inevitably were, with the statesmanlike conduct of his predecessor, Manning.

VAUGHAN.

THE LATEST BRIGHTON WALK.

Pedestrianism has of late been all the rage throughout England. The craze originated with the Stock Exchange walk to Brighton on May Ist, and in the evening of that day the "Evening News," seeing how great had been the success of that sporting event, offered a fifty guinea prize for a " go-as- you-please " race over the saine course. Of course, the value .of the first prize put all amateurs out of court, and a separate race had to be arranged for . them. Between ...three and four hundred entries were obtained for the original race, which came off on Saturday last, but, owing to the inclemency of the weather, only ninety-one starters toed the line on Westminster Bridge at five on

THE START FROM WESTMINSTER BRIDGE. that morning. However, a very good race was seen despite the heavy road, and, as expected, a new record for the journey was ' put up by Leonard Hurst, the well-known long distance runner. Hitherto the record had been held by F. D. Randall, an amateur cf the Finchley Harriers, and his time was 6 hrs. 58 min. 18 sec. This Hurst beat by nearly half-an-hour, completing the dis- tance of 52 odd miles in 6 hrs. 34 ming 50 sec., the second man, H. S. Bell, being nearly forty minutes behind him. There - were in all eighteen prizes and some extra ones, and the race, despite the weather, proved a very great success.

AN ENGLISH CONDUCTOR OF NOTE.

Mr. August Manns, the veteran musical conductor of the Crystal Palace, well merits the proud distinction just bestowed ,upon him by the Oxford University Convocation. He now possesses the degree of Mus. Doc., honoris causa, and upon no one could the honour have been, bestowed to give more general satisfac- tion to the mus- ical world. His ability as a com- poser is pretty widely known, but not so wide- -I.3i' as it deserves, for Dr. Manns, with the inborn modesty of the true genius, has unfortunately preferred to o much to be the interpreter of others to assert- ing his own pro- ductions. His painstaking ef- "-UST MANNS forts with the Crystal Palace Saturday con- certs have been awarded with phenomenal -success. Dr. Manns is a most strenuous man; in fact, few musicians have worked so hard. He has directed concerts, often given daily— at one time two per day—for forty years and more, and this suggests such an array of figures as could hardly be attained by totalling up all the concerts given by half-a-dozen con- ductors put together, But it is not only in the number of concerts directed that he is famous. His name will be handed down for thC artis- tic results attained, the unknown and import- ant works brought to a • hearing, and, not the least, the ever ready 'help. given to our young English composers.

THE PORT OF LONDON

Amongst various controversial schemes which the government stands pledged to pilot through the rough waters of opposition is the Port of London Bill, which is now in the com- mittee stage. THE SIBERIAN MAMMOTH.

The skeleton of the great mammoth re- cently discovered in Siberia, has now been ex- cavated and mounted for exhibition in the Zoological Museum at St. Petersburg. The head of the carcase was exposed by a small landslip on the bank of the Beresowka river. The proboscis and other soft parts of the head were soon eaten by carnivorous animals, and the only remaining tusk was taken out by the natives. •After this damage had been done, the Governor of Jakutsk learned of the dis- covery and arranged that it should be preserved for scientific examination. Dr. Otto Herz, the famous zoologist, happily arrived in good time and has accomplishel the difficult task

FRONT OF THE MANIAIOH DURING EXCAVATION. Df removing the specimen to St. Peters- burg. The carcase was buried partly in ice and partly in frozen earth, which began to thaw as soon as the earth was removed. The most delicate soft parts were in a re- markable state of preservation, and the chief lifficulties arose in preventing the tissues from putrefying when they were taken out. In fact, the skin was so well preserved that it 'las now been stuffed, The skeleton was re- moved and mounted separately, while as many of the soft parts as possible have been !,reserved in various ways in bottles. The skin has lost much of its hair, but some of this has been restored from other specimens in the museum. The animal is a yonng male, and not remarkable for its size.

THE NEW LA.

The election of a Royal Academician is always a subject of considerable interest and -;peculation, especially in artistic circles. Last week a general meeting of the Academicians and Associates was held at Burlington House to fill up a vacancy, and Mr. Aston Webb, the well-known ar- chitect, was elec- ted to the full membership of the Academy. This came as somewhat of a surprice to those supposed to be " in the know." The vacancy was thought to lie between four other A.R.A.'s, and Mr. Aston Webb was scar- cely- mentioned by the prophets. The quartette in .question. were Mr. Solomon J. Solomon, Mr. IL H. La Thangue,

Mr. G. Clausen,

and Mr. Napier Remy; but, as is often the-case, another was preferred to the favourites. Mr. Aston Webb, the new R.A. was born in 1849, and has at- tained great eminence as an architect, stand- ing quite at the top of his profession. He was I•lected an A.R.A. in 1897 ; but what set the seal on his fame was the selection of his design ior the architectural portion of the Victoria Memorial in the front of Buckingham Palace. Amongst his other public works, the better known are the arch for the completion of South Kensington Museum and the Royal College of Science; the French Protestant Church; Soho; the Royal United Service In- stitution, Whitehall; the Royal Naval Col- lege, Dartmouth; the new schools of Christ's Hospital; the new Birmirigham University; the restoration of St. Bartholomew's the Great, EC., and many others. His selection is ad- mitted upon every side to be a good one, and has given general satisfaction.

THE SENIOR WRANGLERS.

The position of Senior Wrangler at Cam- bridge is quite historic, and the name of the first mathematician of his year •is always re- ceived with: considerable public interest throughout the country. This year the results of the tripos examination are unusually in- teresting, as two men have been bracketed in the place of honour by the examiners, who could not separate them, and of them one is a board school boy, who, like Mr. Asquith, and other examples, thereby helps to vindicate our system of universal educa- tion. Mr. H. Bateman commenced his education at the Dacie Avenue Board School in Manchester, from which he passed on to the well-known Grammar School with a scholarship. In two he left here, having won a scholorship at Trinity College, Cambridge,

MR. H. BATEMAN. and his proud position of Senior Wrangler justi- fies the enlightened policy pursued in Man- chester and certain other large towns. Another Trinity scholar of 1900 was, Mr. P. E. Marrack, thz other Senior, who was born at Birkenhead, and educated at Blundell's School, Tiverton. He, too, has had a very distinguished 'Varsity career, and Mr. Bateman and he have carried everything . before them in mathematics among the men of their own year. The only woman Wrangler is Miss Hilda Hudson, "equal to seventh," who is a student at Newnham. She comes of a most distinguished mathematical family. Her father is Pro- fessor Hudson of King's College, London, Third Wrangler in 1861. His brother was Senior Wrangler three years later, and his son held the same proud position in 1898. Another daughter was "equal to eighth'' in 1900, while a third took the highest mathematical honours at Oxford truly a ,wonderful records

"LITTLE ENGLAND BEYOND WALES."

The dampand the varying climate of "Little England 1 beyond Wales," as the county of Pembrokeshire issome- times called, was responsible for the painful experiences I of William Charles, a dairyman, of the Observatory, Hakin, near Milford Haven. He has aroused interest in Pembroke, as he has been known to be a sufferer from i rheumatic fever for very many years, and from its after effects, and experienced a wonderful cure by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people. He said: "Eight or nine years ago I had a most severe attack of rheumatic fever. In spite of all the doctors, I did not rally, and all hope of my complete recovery was practically given up. Youare no doubt aware that rheumatic fever always leaves something behind it, often a weakness of the heart, and that is why assurance companies won't ancept a risk on the life of one who has suffered from the disease. In my case the rheumatic pains became chronic and for seven years I was a confirmed invalid. A neighbour told me of Dr. Williams' pink pills, and in spite of my state, advised me to give them a trial, saying that in cases which all ordinary medicines were powerless to touch, these pills have effected their most astonishing cures. I took the pills after each meal. They are not purgative, but, on the contrary,strengthen- ing, and although I did not feel much good from the first box, my wife persuaded me to persevere with a second. I felt a distinct improvement from that box, and before long the racking pains gradually left me and I was able to take food as I had not been able to do for years. I have never looked back since. The stiffness and pain never troubled me again." A good deal is being said in the daily papers on the practice of substitution by shopkeepers who, when asked for well-known remedies, try to sell worthless

substitutes for the sake of greater profit. It is therefore interesting to note that the pills which cured Mr. Charles were the genuine pills. The genuine Dr. Williams' have *- 1,

THE WESLEY BICENTENARY. CELEBRATION IN WARRINGTON. Interesting Addresses.

Last week saw the two hundredth anniversary of the birth of John Wesley, and it was fittingly com- memorated in all places where "the people called Methodists" (to quote Wesley's own historic phrase) are found. There was a pilgrimage of British and American Methodists to Epworth, the little Lincoln- shire village where Weslby first saw the light, and meetings were held in all parts of the country. In the course of his travels Wesley frequently visited Warrington and recorded his impressions in his " Journal" from time to time. In the year 1757 he preached "about noon" (upon the 2nd of March) "to a wild, staring people, (very few excepted)who seemed just ripe for mischief', though he added "the bridle was in their jaws." In 1760 he preached (again at noon) in the town." Though many of the' beasts of the people ' were Present the bridle from above was in their teeth, so that they made not the least disturbance." After a brief intermediate visit he came again in 1761 "but what a change !" he recorded" No opposer, nor any trifler now ! Every one heard as for life. In 1766 he addressed at War- rington" about noon, a large congregation, rich and poor, learned and unlearned" and he continued in the "Journal," had' never spoke more plain nor seen a congregation listen with more attention." In 1778,the entry ran: "At one I preached in . Warrington, I be- lieve all the younr, gentlemen of the Academy were there ; to whom fstated and proved'the use of reason, from those words of St. Paul, In wickedness be ye children, but in understanding be ye men." Upon the occasion of a. Latervisit ' Wesley concluded that " at last there is reason to hope that God will have a steady people even in this wilderness." Warrington Methodists did not commemorate the bi-centenary on Wednesday,but on Thursday last week Bold-street Wesleyan Church was the scene of a gathering in every way worthy of the occasion. It was attended by the members of Wesleyan congre*a- tions in the Warrington and Frodsham circnits and speeches dealing with various aspects of the great evangelist's work were delivered by ministers who had addressed the great meeting in Manchester on 'Wed- nesday evening. Mr. E. J. Guest, 8.A., j.P., presided and although the chapel was not filled the attendance , was gratifyingly large. The ministers present in- eluded the Revs, John S. Simon (Governor of Didsbury College and chairman of the Manchester district), Trevor H. Davies of Stockport, S. E. Keeble (editor of the "Methodist Weekly "), G. H. Carnson (superintendent of the Circuit), R. Wilkins Rees, John Gunnell, E. Gelder (Frodsham), W. C. J. Neville, and J. Gardham Reed (representing the Primitive Methodists). Representatives of the Independent Methodists were also present, including Councillors James Evans and Barton. The singing was led by a • special choir drawn from the churches in the Circuits, I Councillor Walter Brough conducting. Mr. Frank I Hickman presided at the organ with his usual skill. • Old Methodist hymns were sung with fervour and great enthusiasm prevailed throughout the meeting. THE CHAIRMAN'S ADDRESS. WHAT METHODISM NEEDS TO-DAY.

The CHAIRMAN said he had not sought that position, and naturally he should shrink from it, although he felt it to be a great honour to have been asked to take such a position. If he had any fitness for that chair it was relative rather than personal. He might claim to be in a sense nearer to the times of John Wesley than nerhaps anyone else in that house with the exception perhaps of a few. In his family they reckoned generations not by twenty-five or thirty, but by fifty years, so that if his father were alive to-day he would be 110 years of age. If his grandfather Guest were alive he would be about 160; . so that when his grandfather was born John Wesley was about forty years of age, and just on the threshold of that grand work which he accomplished during over fifty years. That seemed to bring him (the speaker) in a way nearer to the times of John Wesley,only two generations intervening. He had been associated with that house of prayer more or less all his life. (Hear, hear.) Hecouldnot say that he remembered the foundation stone of the building be- ing laid, neither could he remnmber the opening ser- vices but he was intimately associated with the stalwart men who co-operated with the Rev. George Dickenson in the erection of that fine edifice. He need not men- tion the names of all—there were Dr. Smith, senior, Robert Garnett, senior, James Dutton, James Carter, Mr. Bennett, of Padgate, John Guest and many others. These men being dead yet spoke to them of sanctified audacity. It was no mean thing fifty years ago to erect a building like that. These men left them an example which, he feared, they had not imitated. A great deal of work had been done in this circuit during the past fifty years,but it was not commensurate,it seemed to hi m,with the work which was started when that noble edifice was built. There was no one in that house that night who had a more profound admiration for John Wesley that he (the speaker) had. He did not think that the young preacher was irreverent when,wishing to commemorate the work of John Wesley, he gave out as his text "There was a man sent from God whose name was John." John the Baptist was a great and burning light, but John Wesley was even greatair than John the Bap- tist, He did a grand and noble work which survived to this day. They must remember that John Wesley as an Anglician clergyman, a priest of the Church of I England, was in many respects narrow' but as a man sent from God full of the Spirit, following the gleam and guidance of the Divine Spirit, he was a man broad as the world in which he lived. John Wesley as an Anglican priest was narrow and looked with horror at the thought of laymen preach- ing the Gospel; but John Wesley, the man sent from God, reioiced that the finger of God was in that work. j John Wesley, the Anglican priest, would have confined I himself to his Parish Church and would have preached j in no church where he was not invited or permitted to I preach, but John Wesley, the man of God, full of the Spirit,looked upon the world as his parish. John Wesley, the Anglican priest, believed only in three orders of , ministry—Bishops, priests and deacons—and he recog- I nized no man as Bishop who had not been consecrated by three Bishops, neither did he recognize the orders of priest or deacon unless they had been ordained by the imposition of the hands of a Bishop, but John Wesley, the man of God full of the Divine Spirit, saw the necessity for ordaining his own Bishops and ministers and sending them forth to and fro throughout the earth. He (Mr. Guest) was a Wesleyan Methodist to his back- bone. (Applause.) After nearly fifty years of careful consideration he knew of no Church that was so fitted to carry the Gospel throughout this and other lands as was the Methodist Church. He dare not leave the Church of his birth. He could never forget that it was in that sanctuary where the Word of God first reached his heart, where he heard those mighty men of God preach the truth which went to his heart andconscience. j He remembered the sermons or the effect that sermons of such men as G. B. Mellor, John Hannah, Morris, Frederick Wooley, and a great number of others made upon him;and he remembered that it was in that house of prayer that he confessed his sins to God at the penitent form. He dare not look upon that church if he had been a renogade to his church. But it seemed to him and that Methodism to-day was not the Methodismthat itwas in the days gone by. It might be that he was getting old and that he was dwelling now upon the past rather than upon the present but when he thought of the ex- perience of men and women who used to speak from that gallery in the Love Feast in the fervour of their souls, when he thought of the earnest prayers they poured forth, he wondered whether the same spirit ani- mated Methodists to-day,if they, had the same strength buoyancy of hope the same tenderness of life, that the old •Alethodists had. They had organization now, it was true, but they wanted more of the spirit of their fathers and he prayed God, that He might give it to them. He trusted that that meeting would not end in the count- ing of numbers nor in the enthusiasm of the gathering, but that it would be the beginning of better days for Warrington Methodism, that there would be a new start from that night, that they would go on and prosper and that the Lord would be with them. (Applause.) WESLEY, THE EVANGELIST. The first speaker was the Rev. J. S. SIMONS, governor of. Didsbury College and Chairman of the Manchester district. He said it always made his heart glow when a man referred to his Methodist ancestry and declared his attachment to the Methodist Church. He thought he might claim a very honourable Methodist ancestry. He was a Methodist of the fifth generation and a Methodist preacher of the third generation. His great- grandfather was selected by John Wesley himself as one of his preachers and he fNind the strain so good that he. sent his brother into the work. The rev, gentleman proceeded to address the meeting on "John Wesley, the Evangelist" and treated the subject in a highly interest- ing way. He directed his hearer's thoughts to the scene on a mount near Bristol, when George Whit- field addressed the rough Kingswood colliers. John Wesley was present at this memorable gathering and his heart was strangely moved. The speaker pointed out how, after addressing a religious society on the Sermon on the Mount, Wesley was led to follow Whitfield's example, and on the next day, April 2nd, 1739, he preached to 3,000 people on a little eminence near Bristol. From this beginning Wesley went to and fro throughout the nation, preaching deliver- ance to the captives, and opening the prison to those who were bound: (Applause): He often wondered what it was that made Wesley's ministry such a power,and he thought the reason might be found in some of the hymns that they sang—he was almost tempted to say in some of the hymns that they did not sing. The speaker alluded to Wesley's intense faith in eternity, his clear Sight of the Judgment Throne, his immovable belief in the doom of the wicked, and then went on to picture Wesley as the evangelist, standing hidden in the splen- dour of the Cross, but unveiling that splendour to the eyes of all penitent sinners who looked to it yearning for salvation. In speaking of the present day needs of Methodism, he said he detested sneers at culture. Let

them have as much culture as they could bear—and some of them could not bear very much. (Laughter.) Let them have an educated Church—a wealthy Church if they liked. He did not despise a good balance at his bankers if he ever possessed one. (Laughter.) He asked them not to descend to that low cant that derided learning, culture and wealth. Let them have a strong and mighty Church, but first and foremost at the very foundation let them have that passion for the well- being of souls, that wide-hearted, clear-eyed evangelism of John Wesley. (Applause). THE MISSION OF METHODISM.

The Rev. S. E. KEEBLE, in the course of an interest- ing address, pointed out that he came into Methodism from the Anglican Church. He thought he could under- stand the spirit of Methodism quite as well as those who were Methodists of the fifth generation. (Laughter.) There were some people who were Methodists because their fathers were and for no other reason, and he felt sure that if any church or circuit had too large a pro- portion of such, death would speedily ensue. Methodism he declared, could never live with a mechanical Chris- tianity. Unless it had the warm loving: heart of spiritual experience it was no use; it was untrue to its past, impotent in the present and it would not be heard of in the future. re ferring to his doubts as a sceptic in his youth, Mr. Keeble said he had found that Christianity was not a matter of the head, but a matter of the heart. (Hear, hear.) He proceeded to refer to the influence which John Wesley's sermons and journal had on his mind and heart. and howhe was finally led to become a Methodist. Addressing himself to the question of whether Methodism was an extinct volcano, he said Methodism had had its day unless it lived by exactly the same spirit with which it began, and unless it kept its hold upon the prime principles upon which it was founded. "Unless there be in us," he continued, "the same spiritual life the same affection for the name of Jesus, the same profound trust in Him and sweet beautiful surrender to Him, the same passion for personal good- ness and the same yearning desire to get other men to participate in that secret; unless there be in our hearts an enthusiasm for holiness and an enthusiasm for humanity then Methodism must die no matter how many 20th century funds you raise or how many millions you give." Speaking of the mission of Methodism, Mr. Keeble said there were three perils before them, and he thought Methodism was best able to deal with them.. The three things they would have to reckon with were materialism, saterdotalism and socialism.. Materialistic philosophy, he said was born, in England, and we should always produce materialistic philosophers It was nothing but the power of John Wesley that destroyed Deism in this country and the Wesleyan Methodists could destroy the materialism of the 20th century, if they had real spiritual men and women. As for sacerdotalism, he felt sure that the supreme religion of the heart and spirit in a living church possessed of the New Testamentspirit of brother- hood and simplicity and spiritual life would be death to any mechanical religion. Finally they would have to deal with socialism. Had they seen the returns from Germany, the most highly educated nation in Europe ? Did they think that if the Germans, who led Europe in thought, believed so much in socialism. that we English people would never have to face it? We would certainly have to face it and unless we could face it with the principles of Christianity, we should havesome dreadful forms of it in our midst. The truth in socialism would be so attractive to the multitude that the 1119.Sses would swallow the errors and immorali- ties as well as the truth. If you are only true continued Mr. Keeble, to the spirit of Primitive Methodism and preach the gospel to the poor, love the common people and build up a ChriAian democracy then you will have something to cope with socialism. You can extract its truth and its errors will perish. (Applause.)

Customer First

The way to do business is to put one's customer's interest first: be faithful to him. Fels-Napfha does that; it goes far to make a grocer a good one.

Fels-Naptha 39 Wilson street London E C

The litigation between the Corporation and traders of Warrington on the one handand the ManchesterlShip Canal Company on the other has taken a new turn. In the Court of Appeal on Wednesday before Lords Justices Vaughan Williams, Romer, and Stirling, Mr. Moulton, K.C. (with whom was Mr. Clare) applied on behalf of the Canal Company that the appeal from the judgment of Mr. Justice Byrne might be withdrawn and a fresh appeal entered, or in the aljternative that the appeal might be postponed until the next sittings. Counsel said the action had reference to the obligations of the Manchester Ship Canal Company to dredge part of the old bed of the river Mersey below the point where it left the Ship Canal. It was contended on be- half of the plaintiffs that the Company had failed in their duty to keep tilt river dredged so as to allow of , a depth of eight feet at low spring tides. Mr. Justice Byrne, in the Court below, had decided in favour of the plaintiffs, but the order had not yet been drawn up. The defendants desired more time to allow them to consider their position as regarded the advisability of promoting a bill in Parliament. The question was one of very great seriousness, and it might be solved by fresh legislation. No harm whatever would follow the post- ponement of the appeal, and it was their intention to do their best to comply with the interpretation of the Judge. Mr. Bousfield, K.C., for the plaintiffs, said this was a matter of vital importance to Warrington. The ques- tion for the consideration of the Court was not a com- plicated one. It WM as to the liability of the Ship Canal Company to dredge the bed of the river Mersey to a depth of eight feet so as to be available for vesselsat low spring tides. Total 33 Lord Justice Williams: Suppose they go on with their dredging at once? • Mr. Bousfield said that would place the matter on a , different footing. Lord Justice Williams said he understood Mr. Moulton was prepared to go on with the dredging. Lord Justice Williams said it seemed to be hopeless to try to get the matter arranged between the parties, and the Court would deal with it. They directed that the appeal should not be taken for three weeks after the order of the Court below had been completed.

WORDS OF ENOOURAGfEIWENT.

The Rev. TREVOR DA.VIES, of Stockport, pointed out that although John Wesley was a great scholar, a great theologian and a great organiser it was as an evangelist that he had re-created England. (Hear, hear.) After referring to John Wesley's evangelistic tours in all parts of the kingdom, and pay: jug a tribute to his great work, Mr. Davies said theY were not there to idolise a great man. They were there to catch the inspiration of his spirit and his message. He had committed to them a most sacred trust, and that trust was to continue the glorious ministry which he commenced in the 18th century right forward into the 20th century. They were sometimes told that the day of Methodism was over, and that she was sinking back into the source from whence she came. He believed that Methodism was only coming in yet. (Hear, hear.) If they were faithful to the memory and tradition of their church, faithful to their founder, faithful to the men who sacrificed everything to preach the gospel of Jesus Christ, then they would realise that Methodism would do a greater work for England in the 20th century than she was ever able to do in the 19th century. The two great evils that beset the religious life of England to- day were superstition and indifference. Superstition went hand in hand with materialism. They were threatened by a return of enervating ecclesiasticism but as Methodists they were determined that no Italian monk should again govern in the committees and schools and churches in this country. (Hear, hear.) The only way of expelling an untruth was by preaching the truth. (Applause.) The Roman Church conspired to keep the soul away from Christ. What with their bishops, their priests, their sacraments, and their ceremonies, the Grace of Christ had to be filtered through dense masses of ecclesiastical debris. He would ask them to sweep it all away in the name of Jesus Christ. If they brought England into contact with the Lord Jesus Christ they would sweep away these dread- ful spectres of superstition that preyed upon the national mind and threatened to hinder the progress of the English nation. Referring to the chilling indiffer- ence to Christianity which was so generally manifested, Me. Davies said England needed bringing to the vision of Christ as John Wesley saw it. It was a wonderful thing that out of one earnest sonlsuch great good should have come. To-day Southey 's it Aiction that the name of John Wesley would be more Widely known than that of Frederick or Catherine had been fulfilled. Therefore let them be`.;" steadfast, immovable, always abounding in the' Name of the Lord, inasmuch as ye know your work is not in vain." (Applause).

===== 0002601_19030626_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
VOTES to`F THANKS.

The Rev. JOHN GUN-Z.:ELL proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman and speakers, and said the gathering was a memorable one, the occasion was memorAble. He would like to link Mr. Neville's name with the names e had already mentioned. Mr. Neville had had the major part of the toil connected with the organization of the meeting, and had laboured very hard and diligently. The Rev. R. WILKINS REES seconded, the Rev. G. H. CARNSON supported, and the motion was heartily carried. The choir, organist and conductor were thanked for their services, and the Chairman having responded, the meeting concluded with the hymn "All hail the power of Jesus' name."
