THE EXAMINER.

JUNE 26U3, 1903.

AFRICA has, from its BAD NEWS FROM first appearance in AFRICA. history, been a land of uncertainty and 414 aster, and there are many reasons at present ll'hy the mind of this country should be seriously Perturbed in regard to events that are now in pro- gress there. In the House of Commons Mr. CHAMBERLAIN made some attempt to explain the situation in Northern Nigeria, the scene of Sir FREDERICK LUGARD's rapid and unauthorised ex- ploit. The Hgh Commissioner of Northern Nigeria s&zed an erupts which had some preten- sions to antiquity and civilisation with a mere handful of men. It was no doubt a brilliant thing to do, but it does not follow that it was either wise or necessary or justifiable as an act done in furtherance of genuine British interests. General LUGARD was in the first place made the subject of official reproof; but when his adventure seemed to have been brought to so uccessful a conclusion, the Government denied that it had inten led. to zeprove him, accepting, as usual, the accomplished fact. However, it has now been evident for some time past that the affair was very far indeed from having been concluded. After the sudden capture of his capital, the SULTAN of Sokoto fled across Northern Nigeria. He was pursued by Captain SWO'RD and some men of the West African Frontier Porce, who attacked a walled town called Darmi. The attack was unsuccessful, as Captain SWORD had no artillery.gThe casualties on our side wqre tumorous in comparison to the smallness of our force. Here was another " regrettable incident." In fact, the e'-SULTAN, as Mr. CHAMBERLAIN ex- plained, was at the time of the last advices about 400 miles from Sokoto and 200 from Kano, where he is gathering the tribes around him evidently with till intention of giving Us trouble. Sir FREDERICK LUGAR]) has, in fact, Provided us with another "little war," which will require to be waged over an extensive country, and for an object that the Government never seems to have contemplated. This is not the first occasion

on which General LUG' ARD has acted "the prancing Pro-Consul" with a success which has led to some embarraqiment and much expenditure. 11-3 is clearly a man who should be ordered not to go beyond his instructions. Nelson, no doubt, pro- vided himself with a splendid excuse for putting his telescope to the blind eye, but the example is not one for imitation.

In Somaliland we seem lilelv to have another Ladysmith. Mr. BRODRICK had singularly little Information to give on the subject. The latest news came from General MANNING, and was dated June 15th. General MANNING and Colonel COBBE were then starting on a march from Badwin to barnot, which would occupy three or four days. It was their intention to march in two columns, from two to five miles apart, to pick up the garri- son and then retire to Bohotle. Mr. BRODRICK Izlid not think it desirable to give the full strength of the force, which, however, seems too small even to conduct a retreat with 7safety. The retiring columns are, in fact, in great danger. The hills between Da,ruot and Bohotle are occupied by the Mullah's riflernsn. We are confronted not only by an alert but by an able enemy. The Mullah's whole army has been seen defiling past the British outposts at Damot and Bohotle, and the Mullah himself is in a position from which he eau strike either between Damot and Bohotle or between Badwein and Damot. Until we have further news, therefore, there is cause for grixest; apprehension, Our posts are full of sick men Tilltny of our irregular Somali levies have deserted to the Mullah; it is only an uncertain reliance that can be placed ou the others. Yet the Minister for war talks of not sending a lir 4e frei

It seems that there MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S were more than a FOLLOWERS. hundred Unionist members present on Wednesday at a meeting held at the House of Commons to consider MT. CHAMBERLAIN'S fiscal proposals. These may be regarded as Mr. CHAM- BERLAIN'S friends and personal supporters. The resolution at which they arrived was, under the circumstances, of an unexpectedly mild character. They are in favour of inquiry. Well, nobody fears inquiry. What is to be apprehended is the eff.3ct of a sudden and turnultous appeal to prejudice and ignorance, such as is being made by the new Protection League. That system of Free Trade which has placed us at the head of trading and industrial nations will bear looking into rather carefully. The more it is made the subject of inquiry the more conspicuous its merits and services will become. What is odd is that there should be more than a hundred men on the Unionist benches who con- sider an inquiry to be necessary. It is under- stood that these gentlemen, so far as they are prepared to commit themselves, are in favour of a retaliatory tariff on manufacturers rather than of a tax on food. Indeed, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is himself understood to have weakened somewhat as to the advisability of making dearer food and h'gher wages his principal cry. Higher wages mean a higher cost of production, and a higher cost of production means loss of foreign trade. "Increase my costs," says an eminent merchant and manufactnrer, "and shut me out of markets into which I get even now only by the skin of my teeth." Foreign trade means decreased wages or no wages at all. There is no possibility of proving the contrary of this proposition. To inquire into it will only be to establish it more firmly. The fascination of dearer food having failed to appeal to the Unionists who assembled to talk matters over yesterday, a policy of retaliatory tariffs presented itself as something on which a successful appeal could be made to the country. Inquiry into the wisdom of a step like this will necessarily invplve questions as to who is likely to be most injured by retaliation, ourselves or those on whom we retaliate. Free Traders will welcome the most wide and the most minute inquiry On this subject. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN would have the people believe that we are being beaten in the world's markets by the United States and Germany, and more particuLtrly by Germany. But what are the facts? Hardly anything could be further from the truth than the statement that our trade is being diminished in extent by the foreign tariffs that are directed against it. The most that can be said with any accuracy is that foreign tariffs prevent it from being so extensive as it might otherwise become. Oar trade is not diminishing, .it is increasing; and, con trary to a common misrepresentation, it is increasing at a vastly greater ratio than that of Germany. The simple truth is that our lead over Germany in foreign trade is about 130 millions greater than it was sixteen years since. If under these circumstances, retaliation could still be held to be justifiable, what is it that we should retaliate upon? Rghty-two- per cent. of our ' imports consist of food and raw materials, and the remaining 18 per cent. of manufactured articles. It is the foreign manufacturer who is popularly I supposed to be pushing us to the wall, and it is a proposal f w striking at manufactured goods that would most readily find favour with the people, but of our own exports 85 per cent. consist of manufactures which we contrived to 'sell in spite of hostile tariffs, but which Yre should be unable to force into foreign markets if those tariffs were made retaliatory, as they Cer- tainly would Should we be foolish enough to com- mence a tariff war. What Mr. CHAMBERLAIN proposes to present to the people is that our Colonies and depecdencies would take from us a hat the foreigners refused. What, then, is the ground for such an assertion ? Our trade with our colonies is less than one-third of our total ex- port trade. The colonies take from us just as much as they cannot do without. There is not the least reason to believe that they would take more than they do at the present time if they were to strike a blow at the "foreigner. "The colonial tariff," says an Australian newspaper, " is mainly against Great B-itain." "I observe to my surprise, said Mr. CLIAMBERL4.IN to the Colonial Premiers "that Canada has a tariff against us averaging 171 per cent.,"and he asked, flow much of this are you prepared to relinquish? The reply of Canada was that it is prepared to relinquisb nothing. That will be the reply of the other colonies.

A new form of torture came in with the VAT/lc/- phone. That attractive instrument, once corn - mendably indistinct, has now developed its powers alarmingly. One of the London suburbs feels the development keenly. That suburb has a resident who is the proud possessor of a ten horse-power gramophone, with a triple-expansion nozzle. He is not selfish. Such a fine instrument should not he monopolised by one person, he thinks; so he opens his bedroom window,places his gramophone on the sill,with the " trumpet " pointing heavenward,and starts the machine. The devastc:tion he has caused already has alarmed the other inhabitants, and they are loualy inquiring what they can do to stop the nuisance, The suburbs are the peculiar region of nuisances of this kind —the pianistic young lady, the vocalist, an,' other performers go to make up the daily misery of the lan dof small houses and flats. The consequences to the unfortunate neighbours are not pleasant to contemplate, and the bitter cry of the suburban resi4ents is heard in almost every large town. Mr. Gilhert's cure for motorists—a charge of small shot properly placed —would appear to be the only possible remedy,

The heavy blow is one which has not dispensed with the necessity of retreating for the third time before this vigilant enemy. The Government is, according to its habit, "muddling through," which means a policy of drift, in this instance such as has obvi- ously exposed our scanty troops to the danger of annihilation. Whbn the Government drifts it is always in the wrong direction. It drifts into difficulties but does not drift out of them. It appears as if the expedition were just beginning, says the able and experienced corres- pondent of the " Daily Telegraph," and he adds, "there is no knowing how long is will last unless a railway is built expeditiously. Many reasons have been given for the success of the German Social Democrats at the polls. High tariffs, the Kaiser's speeches, the commercial crisis have all been suggested as contributory causes. The main factor in tke tr;umph, however, those conversant with the politics of the Fatherland tell us, is the man at the head of the movement. Herr August Bebel is the one great party leader in modern Europe. He manages his forces in the Reichstag with an ability equal to that displayed by Parnell, and his hold in the country is far greater than that which the Irish chieqain ever maintained. He has intellect, and he has also sympathy. He has culture, which makes his speeches models of eloquence, and he has a voice of singular sweetness. He does not excite his hearers as Herr Liebknect did, but is distinguished by a moderation and reasonable- ness which give conviction. Yet, in his relations with his party, he is firmness • itself. More than once he has used plain language to the German Socialists. When anti-Jewish feeling was at its height in Germany, Babel pleaded for justice, de- claring that if Jews beat Germans in the industrial struggle it was because they had more brains,were more industrious, and more sober. So, again, when Anglophobia broke ou'.. during the Boer War Herr Babel denounced the agitation as a folly and a crime, and told his fallow-countrymen that to turn against England was to court disaster. At

one time Germany had only the prison for su man, but persecution has not embittered him. "Leave Social Democracy to me," said the Kaiser once ; "I will soon make an end of it." So far from being broken,the Socialist party is the strong- est to-day in German politics.

Rumours of an electicn before the year is out fill the air, and the fact that they are so circum- stantially discredited by the ministerial organs lends considerable colour to them. We believe an appeal to the country at a not very distant date to be quite probable, particularly if Mr. Chamber- lain is allowed to run riot on the tariff question. The more he says about protective tariffs for further- ing the unity of the Empire and the more rapidly, will the day of reckoning approach. In the meau- time the Liberal party here ought to be looking to the munitions of war. We do not want to be caught napping through an unexpected Government overthrow, and now is the time for both candidate and party to bestir themselves. Liberalism will have a great opportunity when the election does come and we look for fulli advantage being taken of that opportunity in this constituency

It was but fitting that local Wesleyans should commemorate the two hundredth anniversary of th birth of John Wesley. The founder of Methodism paid numerous visits to Warrington during his evangelistic tours,and although he met with many discouragernents, he was able to write in his journal in his later years " At last there is reason to hope that God will have a steady people in this wilderness." It cap hardly be considered a com- pliment to the " good old town " that the great evangelist described it as a" wilderness," and per- haps there may be some who will be still disposed to question whether Wesley's hope of a "steady people" has been fulfilled in Warrington. Never- theless there is reason to believelthat the good seed which Wesley sowed with such a generous hand fell on fruitful ground in Warrington, for Methodism has become strongly rooted in this circuit and at the present time shows signs of a healthy and vigorous life.

The name of "John Wesley" will ever be held in high esteem by those who have sympathy with Methodism. Many have become so accustomed to the familiar name that the Rev. John Gannett's statement last week that Wesley was baptised "John Benjamin Wesley" must come as a small revelation. Few, however, will regret that "Benjamin" was dropped out of the great evangelist's name. John Wesley is much better, and it is a name that has a sweetness of its own to all Methodists. It has been recently rointed out that the Christian name "John " has been associated with the names of the most prominent reformers in Great Britain. Such names as John Knox, John Milton, John Banyan and John Wesley would almost lead one to bOieve that the name !John has a special virtue of its own.

The Examiner Friday, June, 26, 1903. 2/6

DEATH OF COUNCILLOR BAIRD.

This week we have to record the death of another highly respected townsman in the person of Councillor John -Baird,who passedaway at his residence,lTawthorne Lodge, Kuutsford-road, at twenty minutes to nine on Thursday morning. Tt will be remembered that a few months ago the deceased gentleman was seized with fainting fit during a Town Council debate and al- though he recovered and was able to get about he never quite regained his usual good health. About a fortnight ago be had another attack, and was uncon- scious for some time. It was thought that a sea voyage might be beneficial and last Friday he sailed through Menai Straits and spent the week end in North Wales. He was very ill on the return journey but on reaching home seemed to recover and his condition did not give cause for any alarm. He was in the garden on Wednesday morning and there was then nothing to indicate that the end was so near. At two o'clook in the afternoon he became seriously ill, and Dr. Leadbetter was sent for. The patient, however, never regained conscious- ness, and passed peacefully away on Thursday morn- ing. The cause of death was hemorrhage on the brain. Councillor Baird was sixty-three years of age and was held in high esteem by a large circle af friends. He was a native of Jedburgh, Scotland, and came to War- ington about forty years ago. Ever since that time he had been in the employ of Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Ltd., the well-known brewing firm, and was the oldest servant of the company. After passing through all the departments he obtained the responsible position of senior brewer, and discharged the duties in a'manner which gave entire satisfaction. He was greatly esteemed by the late Sir Andrew Walker, and enjoyed the confidence and regard of the chairman,Sir Peter Walker, the managing director Mr. John "Reid Walker, J.P., and Colonel W. Hall Walker, M.P.

, The late Councillor Baird's services on the Town Council commenced in November, IS9S, when he suc- cessfully contested St. John's Ward against Mr. William Furness, obtaining 712 votes against his opponents' 507. He was again returned in Novem- ber 1901, Mr. Samuel Gonerh being nominated as his opponent but withdrawing from the poll. Councillor Baird was a member of Most of the principal commit- tees of the Corporation and deputy chairman of the Health Committee. He took a great interest in the work and was most regular in his attendance at the meetings. He was an active and useful memher of the committee which had charge of the arrangements for the " send-offs " accorded to the Volunteers who served in the South African campaign and he was also to the fore in the organization of cordial recep- tions en their return.

The deceased gentleman was a prominent Free- mason and-had held various offices, being a member of the Gilbert Greenall Lodge. He leaves a widow, a son, and a daughter with whotn the deepest sym- pathy is expressed in their bereavement. As a mark of respect the flags at the Town Hall and Conservative Club are flying at half-most. The interment will take place in Hill Clifft3 grave-yard 'to-day (Saturday) at 12.15 and the Mayor has asked the members of the Council to attend. Carriages will leave the Town Hall at 11.15.

DISTRAINTS NEAR STROUD.

In connection with the passive resistance movement, the assistant overseer and two men, on Wednesday dis- trained on the goods of the Rev. D. J. H. Carter, Baptist minister; Rev. A. T. Hocking, Corwregationa- list minister Mr. J. H. Smart, coal merchant ; and Mr. F. A. Webster, schoolmaster, at Chalford, near Stroud. RU'CORN LIBRARY COMMITTEE.

The monthly meeting of the above committee was held on Friday under the presidency of Mr. W. Handley. The Librarian, Mrs. Jones, said the estimated expendi- ture for the year was £205. It was based upon an esti- mated income of £212. The following were the de- tails: Salaries, £6O; rent, £36; new books, £36; magazines and newspapers, £3O; binding, £3O: printing and statioriery, £10; and sundries, £lO. Last year they spent more for binding. They estimated £3O for new books and £36 for binding. This time the figures had been reversed. The estimate was approved. it was re- ported that a letter had been received from Mr. Carnegie of a distinctly encouraging character asking for ad- ditional information. It was also announced by the Chairman that the library had attained its majority.

CHESHIRE FARMERS AND THEIR POTATOES

At Runeorn on Wednesday, -Joseph Bostock, aged eighteen years, of Northwich,was charged with stealing 61b. of potatoes from the farm of Joseph Walker, of Halewood Farm, Halton. On Tuesday night Police Sergeant Bennett caught the prisoner coming from a field carrying a bag containing the potatoes. The prisoner said he *a.s employed by Mr. Walker, and had permission to take the potatoes. This was found to be untrue.—The magistrates said there appeared to be an impression amongst farm -labourers that they had the privilege of taking potatoes and other small things as a perquisite, and Mr. Walker ought to make it known this was not so.—As Mr. Walker did not press the case, out of respect for the prisoner's father, the prisoner was bound over under the First Offenders' Act.

RESIGNATION OF A. ARNCORN PASTOR.

The Rev. Joseph Osborne, M.A., has received a very hearty and unanimous call to the p.storate of Commer- cial-street Congregational Church, Northampton. A fortnight ago Mr. Osborne announced his resignation as pastor of Bethesda, and the church meeting that`met to consider it expressed great regret at the step Mr. Osborne had taken, especially as he had stated his determination not to consider it. The church meeting therefore ac- cepted their pastor's resignation with feelings of deep regret. Mr. Osborne has now accepted the call to Northampton and will terminate his six years ministry in Runcorn Sunday August 16th, and take up his new work the first Sunday in September. It is interesting to note that Mr. Osborne will be a stronghold of Con- gregationalism at Northampton there being no less than six churches all of which are in a flourishing condition. When Mr. Osborne's decision was announced at this service the congregation rose and sang the Doxology.

WALKING CoNTEFF AT WIDNES.

On Saturday the employees of Messrs. Bolton and Sons, copper founders, Widnes, indulged in a 10-mile walk for prizes, the course lying in the direction of Rainhill, returning through Bold. There were some forty entries, but only twenty-five started and fifteen finished. The afternoon was beautifully fine, and very large crowds assembled to witness the start and finish, whilst crowds assembled at various points. A large number of cyclists and two or three waggonettes also made the round. One of the competitors -was ridden down and injured by an over-ardent cyclist. The results were as follows :-1, J. Knight, time,lhr. 32min.; 2, M. Dunn, lhr. 38min. 5050 c.; 3, B. Locke, ihr.3Bmin. 50sec.; 4, L. Jones, lhr. 41min: 40sec.; 5, H. Ratcliffe, lhr.. 44min. 30sec.: 6, S. Moss, lhr. 45min. 45sec. Prizes were also offered for competitors over 45 years of age, the winners 'being :-1, J. Sparks; 2, R. Parry. Prizes for those under twenty were taken by (1) S. Moss and (2) G. Allison, whilst a "novelty" prize for the last man home under two and a half hours was taken by E. Roberts: All finished under two hours, the actual dis- ance being a little short of the advertised ten miles.

Pollee Court and Prison Gate Mission.

lln Support of this useful institution a meeting was held in Kendrick-street schools, on Monday evening, Ibut was only sparsely attended. The Vicar of St. Paul's (Rev. W. Bracecampl presided, and said the Police Court and Prison Gate Mission was a work which was not fully realized in Warrington. Mr. Taylor, the local missionary, was doing a splendid work, and had been doing for a long time, in helping the fallen. (Applause.) Mr. Henry Thornton said there was great need for carrying on the work. If they 'had had experience and had been in the back slums and courts and alleys of Warrington, amid the many scenes of Misery and wretchedness, they could not fail to be impressed. Almost the sole cause was I drink. There were thousands in Warrington wit, needed the helping hand. The society were lucky in having hit upon a man like Mr. Taylor to take up the Warrington work. (Applause.) He (the speaker) intended to devote the residue Of his life to the cause. The expenditure of the Warrington , branch for last year was £ISG ..135., and the income £lO2 175.. so that there was a balance 'against them of ;£53 168.-1-The Rev. J. Hirst, of Liverpool, also spoke.:—Mr. Taylor, the Warrington Police Court illissionary, in referring to the numarobs domestic matters on which he was consulted, said that last year Ihe had 75 cxses in which the parties came to him 'instead of going to the Police 'Court. Often he and IMr. Thornton visited homes where the people were in dire straits, Without fire, furniture, and clothing, and in.ench cases lie did not know how he could manage without the help and support he received from Mr. Thornton. RUNCORN BOUNDARIES EXTENSION SCHEME.

'On Tuesday morning at the Runcorn Technical In- stitute an inquiry was held by the Cheshire Chanty Council represented by Messrs. J. Thompson, \V. Hodg son and C. IL Booth with Mr. R. Potts (clerk to the County Council) into the application of the Runcorn Urban Council for power to extend the boundaries to include the parishes of Halton and Weston. The peti- tion was opposed .by the Runcorn Rural District Council and the parishes of Halton and Weston. Mr. S. Moss appeared for the Urban Council ; Mr. Sutton, Manchester, the Rural District Council ; Mr. Routledge, Liverpool, Halton and Weston; Mr. J. L. Trafford,the Weaver trustees. The Castner-Kellner works, the Salt Union, the Weston Quarries, Sir it. Brooke, Marquis of Cholmondely, and Major Orred were represented, also other local industries and gentlemen. There was a large attendance of Runcorn, Halton and Weston ratepayers. Mr. Nloss, instating the case for the Runcorn Council, said the parishes of Weston and Halton both adjoined the urban district. Weston formed the area of the water supply of the district. The waterworks were purchased by the Urban Council under their act of 1893, and the Council supplied the parishes of Weston and Halton. By the Liverpool Water Improvement Act the Runcorn authority had power to demand from the Liverpool Corporation not less than 300,000 gallons per day and not more than 1,000,003 gallons per day for the con- venience and the consumption of the inhabitants of the three areas, Runcorn, Weston and Halton. At Weston there were large works, and one of the works was partly in the Parish of Weston and partly in Runcorn. The lower portion of Weston was by arrangement drained into the Runcorn sewers. In another portion of Weston there was a population of 1,030, and this district was not drained at all. That was a very unsatisfactory con- dition of things. With regard to Halton the state of affairs was not mach better. On the lower side. of Halton there were 128 houses with a population of 557, and these houses were not sewered at all, the drainage going into the brook and Ship Canal. Mr. Moss also pointed out that the two districts in question were not adequately lighted. If the amalgamation scheme was granted, the Urban Authority would provide a proper sewerage scheme, and light the townships in a thoroughly satisfactory manner. For some time the manufacturers in the district had wanted a soft water, and Runcorn, owing to special powers, was in a position to provide the Vywrney water and would do so if Weston and Halton came in. There would be a differential rate of 2s. for ten i years, and this Mr. Moss considered would not be any more-7-it might, in fact, be less—than they were paying at the present time. If the townships attempted to deli with the water, sewage, lighting and other questions: the financial burden would be much greater than if Runcorn were to deal with them in a united scheme.

Mr. Marshall, secretary to he Urban Cotmcil,was the first witness and gave detailed evidence respecting the financial position of the town, loans, rates, income and expenditnre.

dross-examined by Mr. Sutton, he said the hospital was insufficient in the view of the County Council, and for the purposes of Runcorn they had been asked to enlarge it. The County Council would pay one-third, and the rest would have to be paid for by loans during the next ten years. The smallpox hospital was not approved by the medical officer of the county. The Council did not contemplate making public baths, and they had no refuse destructor. They had a tip on the Bridgewate- Canal and the rnfuse was taken into the country. In regard to the Fl .e Brigade, Mr. Marshall said the engine was bought 20 or 30 years ago. Dealing with general subjects, he said their present water supply was not good for commercial purposes, and the Council had received complaints from the Rural Dis- trict Council about it. They proposed to pay the Liver pool Corporation Gd. per 1,000 gallons. In answer to Mr. Routledge, witness said the Council had invested £lO,OOO in the Widnes and. Runcorn Transporter Bridge Company. They had borrowed the money at a rate of £3 12s. 6d. If this application was granted the Council proposed to put into operation their powers with the Liverpool Corporation in regard to the water supply. It was proposed that Halton and Weston Point would be supplied with the Liverpool water, and the rest of Weston and Runcorn would be supplied with the Runcorn water and Liverpool water mixed.

Mr. W. H. Hunter, chief engineer to the Manchester Ship Canal, was the next witness, and stated that his company were the largest ratepayers in Runcorn, and he had been authorised by the directors to support the scheme. There were 'opportunities for the development of the Canal at Halton, but there wer3 objections to the insanitary effluents, if these effluents were removed the chief obstacal would be removed.

In reply to Mr. Sutton, witness said the Runcorn water was not so bad as had been stated. It was "utter rot" to suggest that the salt got into the Runcorn water supply from the Ship Canal. _ _ _ .

Tn answer to Mr. Routledge, witness said there were 300,000 tons of sewage put into the river Mersey every year, and there wa.s no authority in existence to prevent it.

Mr. Thompson: Could not the Local Government Board interfere? •

'Witness: The Local Government Board have no authority where salt water flows. The whole of the sewage of Liverpool, Garston, Widnes, and Warrington is emptied into the Mersey, and the same on the other side of the river.

Mr. P. Wilding, surveyor to the Urban Council. next explained the system of sewage in Runcorn. and showed how the sewage of Halton and Weston could be dealt with in a united scheme. The cost of connecting the Liverpool water with that of Runcorn would be .£3,000. The cost of additional hospital accommodation would be about £1,200. The new road to Weston fronaißuncom had been talked of for many years' but was not yet finished awing to disputes between the two authorities. If the land had to be bought and the road completed the cost would probably be £lO,OOO. Mr. Wilding was ques- tioned with a view to showing that Runcorn had done nothinr, towards the development of Weston, but that the trade of Weston had been kept going by the Weaver Canal.

The inquiry at this stage concluded the first day's sitting, and the Commissioners intimated that it would be re-opened on July 14th. •

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

ACCIDENT AT THE VULCAN FOUNDRY.

An accident occurred at the Vulcan Foundry, Ea rle, town on Monday when a youth named John Worrall residing at Golborne, received serious injuries. It appears that Worrall was leaving one of the work- shops at the dinner hour when he ran into some iron plates, which were on a waggon standing in the yard. The sudden concussion causedhim to reel backwards,and he fell heavily on some other iron plates, which were laid on the ground. He was at once picked up and re- moved to the Warrington Infirmary, where he lay for some time in a precarious condition. He is now pro- gressing favourably.

DRUNK AND DISORDERLY.

At the Newton Occasional Court, on Monday, before Mr. J. L. Wood, John Havey, a collier, residing at Lime- street, Haydock, was charged with having been drunk in Swan-road, Newton Common. on Saturday. P.C. Clarke found Havey lying helplessly drank, for which a fine of 2s. 6d. and costs was inflicted.—Robert Samuel Reader and Isabella Reader, of no fixed address, were charged with having been drank and disorderly on Satur- day in Derby-street, zEarlestown. The prisoners were found drunk, cursing and swearing, and on the way to the station the male prisoner became very violent. The female defendant had a melodeon, and on a card hung round the man's neck was the inscription "nearly blind." In default of paying 2s. Gd. and costs, both defendants went to gaol for seven days.—Patrick Lynch, of Burtonwood, was fined 2s. (Id. and costs for having been in a state of intoxication at Earlestown on Saturday.—Patrick Cunningham and John Flynn, two colliers, hailing from Haydock, were mulcted in 2s. 6d. and casts each, for having been drunk and behaving in a disorderly manner at Earlestown on Sunday.—P.C. Mellor proved the case.

ST. HELENS.

ST. • HELENS FATHER SENT TO GAOL.

At St. Helens, on Tuesday, Charles Farnen, a stalwart young man, was sentenced to three months' hard labour for deserting his wife and child. Relieving officer Cooper said this was a most heartless case.

ST. HELENS ACCIDENTS.

Charles Pockey, aged seventeen, of Sutton, was admitted into the St. Helens Hospital on Wednesday, from the Collins Green Colliery, suffering from injuries to the eye.—Graham Dickinson, aged two years, was also admitted to this institution suffering from a broken leg.--John Gorman, aged fifty-eight, of 242, Boundary- road, has also been admitted suffering from injuries to chest and ribs, sustained in connection with the excava- tion at the Peasley Cross bridge.

===== 0002601_19030626_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
WELL-KNOWN ST. HELENS FARMER FINED.

John Littler, farmer, of Fold's Farm, Windle,was fined .E 1 and us. 6d. costs at St. Helens on Tuesday for using three 561 b. weights which were unjust. Inspector M'Donald said he found Mr. Littler weighing oats for market with these weights. They had not been adjusted for years—one not for ten years. Littler was also charged with using an 81b. weight. Mr. Wilson, prose- cuting solicitor, said that the Act of 1890 abolished the 81b. weight, and it was illegal to use it. The chairman said the weight would be confiscated and the case dismissed. _ MISTAKEN IDENTITY AT ST. HELENS.

At St. Helens County Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Superintendent Baxendale, .of Prescott, had a most unusual request to make to the magistrates when he withdrew a charge of riding a bicycle on the footpath at Bold which had been brought against John Jones, a colliery knocker-on at Collins Green. On Whit Sunday Police-constable Howells saw a man whom he believed to be Jones riding on thelfootway in Gorsey-lane. He served the summons personally, and Jones accepted it without denying that he was the offender. When the case came to bp tried on the 9th inst. Jones on oath said he was at Cleethorpes on Whit Sunday, but he admitted that he rode that way on the Monday follow- ing. The case was adjourned for inquiries, but the constable found that he had made a mistake, and he was very sorry for it. Jones had evidently been for some time under the impression that he was summoned for the Monday, and this had contributed to the mistake, which they regretted.

Homing.

The fourth old-bird race in connection with the Warrington and District Homing Society took place on Monday from Jersey, 22 members sending 159 birds.. They should have been liberated on Saturday, but Mr. Taylor' the convoyer, wired that the weather was unfit, and held them over until Monday, liberat- ing them at 5.15 a.m. in a light east wind. - Mr. W. Lamb, Old-road, proved to be the winner, clocking his bird at 11.25, it taking six hours ten minutes for the journey of 292 miles. This is the best record yet done to Warrington from Jersey. THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

• It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly beneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time,' injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they. may injure another. Now the great need of the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the prevalent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adapted to the needs of the mer- chant and the workman, the professional man, and he who wins his broad by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, such a preparation as contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, the Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has failefd to give rerief, and we may say further, that it has pioved permanently beneficial, when other pre- parations at best only gave temporary relief. It is strongly recoinmended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache, Pains in the Side, Giddiness, Loss of Appe- tite, alsofor Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleep- lessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits and all kinds of Weakness. It has often proved very beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness, either after an illness, long confinement to ill-ventilated rooms or any other cause. It strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts as the system which have been weakened by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it is a Natural Tonic, and suitedto all ages at all seasons of the' year. It is everywhere acknowledged to be a powerful aid in restoring the body to a healthy state when deranged by disease. If you suspect that your health is beginning to fail, brace up your nerves, and fortify your constitution by taking now a course of this excellent Tonic, which is sold everywhere in bottles at 2s. 9d. and 4s. 6d. each, but should any difficulty be experienced in procuring it, the Proprietors will forwardosame ,for the above prices. carriage free. Avoid Imitations. When purchasing,see the name, " Gwilym Evans," on Label,Stamp,and Bottle. Sole Proprietors: Quinine Bitters Manufacturing Com- pany, Limited, Llanelly, South Wales.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

Y 0 UTH FUL TH lEV ES AT WARR r.s.TG TON. -Y ACHT BROKEN INTO.

Three small bays named Henry and Samuel Chilton, Dallam-lane, and James Blakeway, Dutton-street, ap- peared before Mr. F. Monks and other magistrates at Warrington Police Court on Monday on the charge of stealing from a skip in the Market-place on Friday night three pinafores of the value of three shillings, the property of Thomas Spencer, draper, 15, Hoyle-street.--The Chief Constable said that at 6.30 on Friday evening the prosecutor left at his stall a large skip which was secured wita the usual iron rod. At 9.20 the following morning he found the skip had been broken into, the rod having been taken out, and the pinafores were missing. Information was given to the police and it was found that the boys had been in the market and had been dealing with the stolen pinafores. The brothers Chilton made a present of one of them to a girl named Mary McLonghlin, another was found in the possession of one of the boys, and another was found in Dallam-lane, singular to say by the mother of one of the prisoners. At least that was her statement and there was no evidence to the con- trary. Mr. Talbot pointed out how frequent robberies from the market were and said that at almost every Court there were cases of that form of theft.—The chairman asked if the stallholders were not in some degree contributors to the robberies by leaving goods in the market over night. Chief Constable said he would ask for a pronouncement on that subject from the Bench He had the greatest sympathy with the stallholders who had great dirficulty to contend with. They were robbed end and side and only about one case in every five detected else; !was brought,before their worships owing to the loss of time to the stallholders which would be necessitated—Mr. Spencer gave evidence and admitted that there was no padlock on the skip. The lock had been broken off in the train.—The chairman thought the prosecutor was placing temptation in the way of thieves by leaving the skip unlocked.—The Chief Constable said Mrs. Chilton neglected her children. They were sent out to hawk matches and were about the streets at all hours of the night. Henry had been before the court previously for stealing from the market and had been birched and imprisoned for a day. —Mr. W. Jones, chief School Attendance Officer, said the boy was at present out on licence from a truant schoel.—The Bench sent Henry Chilton to a reformatory for five years, and ordered the other boys to receive six strokes each with the birch.—The-Chairman referred to the undesirability of property being left in the Market over night in the cpndition that had been mentiened. Either the authorities ought to make proper provision for goods te be left there, or the owners ought to take care of them, and not leave them in the Market all night.—The Chief Constable agreed.—Two other boys named Enos Woods, 50, Plumpton-street, and Thomas Fellows, 51, PlumptOn-street, were then charged with. breaking into a yacht which was lying in the Mersey near Atherton's Quay, and stealing a looking-glass, four lead weights, a plane, and a. fishing- rod, value Sc., the property of William McClew.—The Chief Constable said the yacht was modred near the forge in hallow water. Mr. McClew left it at three o'clock on Thursday afternoon and everything was secure on board, the hatchway being fastened down. When the vessel was visited on the following day it was found that it had been subjected, to piracy if not on the high seas, at all events on the river Mersey. The hatchway had been broken open and the articles named stolen from the cabin.lnformation was given to thvolice and it was ascertained thatthe boys had been dealing with the stolen property. A boy named Hugh Payton saw the prisoners wade out to the yacht, board her, and go into the cabin, aryl subgeitilntly saw the articles in their possession.—Woods was seeteeced to six strokes with the birch rod and Fellows to nine.

ANOMALIES OF REPRESENTATION.

LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE'S ELECTORAL ROLLS.

• The return which has just been laid before Parliament of the number of electors in the nonst'tnencies of the United Kingdom is of considerable interest as it affects the representation of Cheshire and Lancashire. Dealing first with the County Palatine, it may be noted that there is now in the County an aggregate of 692,464 electors, an increase of 10,553 over 1902. As the County possesses 57 Parliamentary representatives there is on the avesage an electorate of 12,148 per member. The average for the Kingdom is 10,506 per member, and on this basis Lancashire is entitled to nine additional members.

There are in Lancashire nearly 50,000 more electors than in the Metropolitanboroughs (including West Ham and Woolwich), but while London has the 61 members to which her electorate entitles her, Lancashire has only 57 instead of 66. Yorkshire, the other great manufac- turinc" county, is also much under-represented, being entitled, like Lancashire, to nine more members. It is interesting to compare these groups of English constit- uencies with the electorate of Scotland and Ireland res- pectively. Scotland has 692,855 electors, practically the same as Lancashire, but she has 70 members instead of 57. This gives an average of 9,898 electors per mem- ber, as compared with 12,148 in Lancashire. Ireland has an electorate of 707,208, with 101 members, an average of 7,002 per member. To enable our readers more easily to compare the figures, we append the totals of the five groups of constituencies we have named, and in the last column show the number of members to which each would be entitled on the basis of equal representation : Group. No. of No. of Average Members members. electors. per entitled

member. to: Lancashire Yorkshire 52 ... 644,064 ... 12,386 ... 61 Metropolis Scotland Ireland The electorate of Ireland, like the population continues to decline. Scotland took the lead in population at the last census. and in a year or two will also have more electors than Ireland. But there is a wide differ- ence in the representation of the two countries, and year by year the disproportion becomes greater. If Lancashire were represented on the same scale as Ire- land, it would have 99 members instead of 57. The number of voters for the present year in the Cheshire constituencies is 141,945, as compared with 139,832 last year4This gives an:average per member of 10,919, which is somewhat in excess of the average for the Kingdom. . Great as are the differences in the representation of the various portions of the Kingdom, the discrepancies between individual constituencies are even greater. In Yorkshire the constituenoies vary from 18,116 at, Middlesbrough to 3,22 a at Pontefract. In the metro- politan and home counties district the smallestconstitu-. ency is St. George in the East with 3,253 electors, and the largest the Rcenford Dieision with 39,158. In Ireland the totals range from 1,517 at Kilkenny to 16,484 at East Belfast, and in Scotland from 2,630 at Wick to 18,569 in North East Lanark. Or if we take a more general view, we find that 27 of the smaller constituencies in the Kingdom, with 27 Par- liamentary representatives, have an aggregate electorate of .only 82,787 electors, while three single-member con- stituencies in greater London have 94,862 electors, though only possessed of a ninth of the representation. As might be expected, every year's delay in redistribu- tion accentuates these glaring inequalities in representa- tion. If the electorate of the small borough and county constituencies does not actually decrease, it increases so slowly that the more rapid growth of the populous centres places them relatively still further ahead. In Lancashire and Cheshire there are no such extreme variations as we have been noticing, but there are inequalities which are sufficiently serious, and theeeare becoming more marked year by year. The largest single- member constituency in the County Palatine is the Stretford Division with 21,415 electors, and the smallest Ashtob-under-Lynewith 7,986. There are eleven con- stituencies (represented by twelve members)which have electorates exceeding 15,000 per member,and an aggregate of 208,192 voters. At the other end of the list there are Lancashire constituencies returning 22 members to Parliament which have an aggregate electorate of only 206,347. In other words the 206,000 voters in the one group have nearly double the representation of the 208,000 in the other. In the one ease the • avezage is 17,168 per member and in the ,other 9382. With the exception of Oldham and Burnley, all the constituencies with more than 15000 electors are county divisions. Oldham, with two members, has more electors than Salford, which is a three-cornered constituency. Liver- pool has orie more member than it is entitled to on a numerical basis, while in Manchester the Average is above that of the country., In Cheshire the largest constituency is the Wirrall Division, with 16,993 voters. Birkenhead comes next with 15,816, and the Altrincham and Crewe Division, follow with upwards of 14,000 in each. It is to be noted that Chester, Stalybride and Stockport(2) which return four members to Parliament have anaggregate of only 27,655 electors, an average pm: member of 6914; whereas the four large constituencies we have named have an electorate of 61,577, an average of 15,394. The Examiner, Friday, June 26, 1903.

matter, and he was afraid what he had to say would be somewhat disjointed. The reison for starting this school was that they had in town such a large number of children who were attending the sixth, seventh, and ex-seventh standards, for many of whom no proper pro- vision was made for teaching. In some cases the children ware taught by uncertificated teachers. When a child got to the age of ten or twelve —this proposal would only deal with those who had reached the age of twelve and did not exceed 16—one would think he or she ought to have the best qualified teachers it was possible to get and not leave them in the hands of uncertificated teachers. In addition, there was no special course of instruction, as far as they had been able to ascertain, which was arranged exclusively for those scholars; and, therefore, they were doing little else than what might be described as "marking time." They were not making that progress that they ought to do and as parents had a right to expect, seeing they were paying fees and the children were being kept at school for that particular purpose. The proposed secondary day school would deal exclusively with those children. It was proposed to educateloo boys and girls. They were restricted. to 100 because the present Tech- nical School would not accommodate more in addition to the pupil teachers being taught there,and he thought the total number of children ip town available for this school amounted to about 1,300, who were qualified by age and having passed certain standards. They were only providing for 100 out of 1,300 who ought to have that provision made for them. (Hear, hear.) The idea was that the brighter and sharper boys and girls would be drawn to this school from the existing ele- mentary schools, and they would be able to get a better average than obtained in the existing elementary schools. They would get the best boys and girls to- gether, and arrange a special course of instruction which would take them on further than they could pos- sibly be taken by being left in the elementary schools. In the latter they could easily imagine there was a cer- tain average, but there would be a few with brighter intellects and sharper brains, and it was those for whom they were trying to cater. There were at the present time as many as 25 children going from Warrington to Widnes for the education they could not get in War- rington, but which would be provided for them if this scheme was approved. In order to restrict the numbers they proposed to have au entrance examination,and only those who passed best would form the .100. It should be restricted also to those who had previously passed throug_h three years at an elementary school in Warring- ton. It was intended to prevent any outsider taking advantage of it, because the accommodation was so small that kp ought to be confined exclusively to rate- payers in tEe town. The reason for adopting a secondary school rather than a higher elementary school was because the age limit in a higher elementary school was restricted from 10 to 15. Ten seemed young,and twelve was a better age. Another reason was that they had the accommodation. It would require some additional teachers or masters appointing, who would also be able to help in the work of carrying on the Technical Classes and Commercial and Continuation Schools. They hoped to be able to get a better selec- tion, perhaps, of teachers, by finding them more complete occupation during the day and even- ing. They could devote their time between the two, as against teachers who merely came to one particular class and subject, and most of whom came from towns outside Warrington, and did not live in Warrington. The proposal would meet a distinct want in the town. It would enable the committee to experi- ment and see how far that want was likely to be appre- ciated, and it would give them experience which would be available in co-ordinating a scheme of education so as to provide a ladder which would enable boys to go through elementary schools to the Grammar School and on to the University. There was no probability of there being any competition between this school and the Grammar School. One would not do anything to interfere or injure the Grammar School. These classes would appeal to an entirely different class of people and children—the class who did not consider the Grammar ,School. No decision had been come to in regard to the fee, but a sum had been suggested of is. per week, pay- able by each scholar. It would be a decided benefit to the pupil teachers to be taught along with the bright boys in the elementary. schools. This was the sort of thing the inspectors had for some time been asking for. The school would, he thought, in a large measure be self-supporting. The 100 children were estimated to earn an average of 80s. per annum' making £4OO. (Hear, hear.) The pupil teachers would be available for this school, and altogether the two would work very well together, especially in conjunction with the teachers of the Technical Institute. The committee, on the whole, had every confidence in putting this forward as being the right thing at the right time. (Hear, hear.) Since coming into that room it had been suggested that the matter be postponed for a month. His objection to that was that it meant they would have to reconsider the question in July, then they would have to wait till August for it to be confirmed by the Council, and it would be impossible to start in September. If they did not start now it meant a delay of twelve months, and it seemed a shame and hard lines on those parents who were anxious to keep their children at soheol that that should be the case. Councillor UPsoN seconded.

Councillor CRIIK moved as an amendment that the minute be referred back. He said he did so for two reasons. The matter of the proposed secondary day school was only brought before the School Management Committee at the meeting held on Thursday last, at which only half of the committee were present, and at the tail end of a meeting with a long agenda; there- fore it had not been discussed by the committee. His other objection to the proposal was that to start the secondary day school in the institute meant getting in the thin end of the wedge for a school ,which was pro- posed, but which the Town Council threw out some months ago. He did not think the school was at all necessary at present; in fact, the matter had not been discussed by the SchoollManagement Committee as a whole, and it was, therefore, premature to bring it before the General Committee. He moved as an:amend- ment thlt the minutes of the School Management Committee be confirmed with the exception of the one relating to the school.

The Rev. E. J. WtI.LCOCKS seconded, and said he felt they were all agreed they ought to have some sort of a continuation school. He was quite open to conviction; if he could be convinced a certain school was right he should agree to it, but 'ho deprecated the haste with which this important step in their policy—this first step in their educational policy—had been rushed through. It was rushed through at a meeting held last Thursday at a small meeting of Ilve. Alderman MONKS: Sir.

The Rev. E. J. WILLCOCKS: STot when the voting wits on. Continuing, he said that the matter was rushed on at the end of a long and weary agenda. He Was sick and weary, and wanted to get away. Why was not the matter brought before a proper meeting, when they could discuss itand think about it? He did,not say that he would oppose the formation of the school, but he thought they should have a higher elementary school with an education of a general and useful character. It was continuous and caroled on the education from the elementary school; and the most important point was that it could he carried onat a low fee or as a free school if they wanted it. Was it right to restrict the school to 100 boys and girls? If they weie te carry out the suggestion, they ought to do so properly, and build a school. It would be it great pity to see the Technical School turned into a school like thetone proposed, as he did not think it was suitable. The pupil teachers were there and the evening classes; and they would be quite enough. He should also like to ask what was the lowest fee that could be charged in a secondary day school. What about the grants? Would they receive grants from all the pupils or only from those who were taking science? What would they teach boys from 12 to 14 years of age? The suggestion seemed to him to be a bit of a farce, and he thought it would be better to give the children a good general education instead of all this science. What would they do with boys of 12 who were no good at science?- They really wanted a practical, higher elementary school for boys until they could do some science. He was not antagonistic to higher educa- tion, but the scheme ought to have been brought before them in such a way that they could have discussed it. He did not think they should be led blindfold, and he must enter protest at that point. This was the first piece of policy they had had before them. (Hear,hear.) Councillor UPSON objected to the charge which had been made that the matter had been rushed. All the members of the committee were summoned. They met at half-past five and it was half-past nine when heleft. The subject was thoroughly discussed, and yet a mem- ber of the committee who ought to have been present took exception to what had been done, moved an amendment, told them that the scheme was proposed at a small meeting, and that it had been rushed. He (the speaker) should strenuously oppose any policy of de- lay. In Warrington they had nearly 70,000 people de- pendent for this higher education on a borough of 30,000. It was perfectly monstrous that boys should have to go to Widnes to get a higher elementary educa- tion that could not be provided in Warrington. Our national existence depended not so much upon the army and navy or even preferential tariffs. (Laughter.) The first line of defence was their education. (Hear, hear, and appktuse.) They had been lax for many years in Warrington in not providing that secondary education they ought to have provided, and which the ratepayers were demanding. Mr. Willcocks had harped very much upon the opinion that that was practically a school of science,but he should like to remind him that there would be religious instruction, French, Latin, mathematics, geometry, modeland free hand drawing, woodwork for boys, geography, mensuration, heat, magnetism and electricity, &c. It was, in fact, an all-round education and not the development of a scientific education; the science imparted would be of a general character, and was not specialized at all. The grants would be paid on the average attendance of the boys in the secondary school, exactly as they were in the primary school, and with regard to the fee, they could if they:thought proper make it a free school. The fee charged at Widnes was 6d., and also at Mancheqter. with a shilling to those living outside the city. The question of the fee need not be considered; it MS not prohibitive at all. and he implored the members of the Committee to hesitate no longer in the establishment of this school. It was one of the crying needs of the town. Where did the girls come in? It was one of th 3 complaints of the Training College authorities that young women came partially prepared for the higher education to be given to them, and this would be to a certain extent a train- ing college for the pupil teachers.

IMr. A. J. JOLLEY Said it seemed to him that the seconder had practically answered the mover of the amendment. Tney contradicted one another, but they had one thing in common, namely, that they wanted to send the matter back for further consideration. The arguments Mr. Craik had used were practically the crusted Tory arguments of 100 years ago, that the three R's were good enough for anyone in Warrington. (Laugh- ter.) He trusted that the majority of the committee woald not go behind the times, but that they would go ahead. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Willcozirs had said that this was an important step forward.He had a strong liking for Mr. Willcocks and always had had,and,therefore,he should ba sorry to say anyticing that would hurt his feelings but he was taking a decidedly backward step instead of a forviard one that evening. The necessary additions to the staff wauld be partly employed in teaching, if the minute was pa,s3ed, in the secondary school and some would be engaged part of the time in the evening chsses, the technical classes pure and simple. They would also have a chance of getting a better class of teachers and better results all through. Councillor ' Craik appeared to look with horror at the prospect of such a school for the benefit of the children, but he (the speaker) welcomed it. One of the most unfortunate steps the Town Council took was when they refused to complete the originalplans for the institute. The build- ; ing was a great blessing to the town at it was, but it would have been a further blessing if it had been corn- ' pleted, and he hoped that not many years would elapse ; before it was completed and crowded with students! drawn from all classes. (Hear, hear.) The members of the School Management Committee had been working • as hard as possible with the sole end of improving • education all lamed, and he hoped that the Education Committee by an overwhelming majority would show thtt they approved of the lines on which the School Management Committee were endeavouring to work. (Hear, hear.) Alderman MONKS said there would be no restrictions or regulations as to the fees; they could fix them at any sum they liked. The Rev. E. J. WILLCOCKS : What do you do with children who don't take science? Suppose the inspector does not pass them. Alderman MONKS: The grant is paid on the attend- ance, irrespective of what is taught, and we can arrange our own course of instruction as seems to be required., We can take certain groups of subjects. There is more I freedom allowed than would be the case in .the higher I elementary school. Mr. T. MAY supported the amendment, and advocated giving opportunities to the yauth of Warrington such as he had in Edinburgh 40 or 50 years ago. Since that time the basis of education in Warrington had been broadened, the scene had been changed, but certain gentlemen were trying to take on their shoulders work that should have been done by the public of Warring- ton and was now being done by the present committee. lie thought they should endeavour to follow the lead! of the Government in that respect and use all the branches of 'education and hasten slowly—it was a good motto " festina lente"—as the work could be done quite as effectually and as efficiently by means of a short ' delay. He did not want secondary education to be delayed; he wanted it to be hastened and advanced, but at the same time, he wanted the members of the com- mittee to be unanimous. With the rates in their present condition the public were not prepared to undertake any large expenditure for secondary education. With a short delay and more enlightenment, they would get the work done betterfand more unanimously andeffectually. The Rev. MORLEY STEVENSON contended that this was a matter of very great importance. Alderman Monks had alluded to a class of children who now formed the upper classes in their elementary schools, and he (the speaker) should like to add to that, a plea on behalf of another class of children for whom it was more import- ant still to provide. Whena child had passed through the elementary school, and when his parents were Successful enough to wishhim to have a year or two more schooling, it was true that there was really no place absolutely in Warrington to which he could go. They had their excellent Grammar School, of which they wereall proud, but it was obvious that the majority of children had not followed the curriculum which would fit them for a Grammar School education or be able to pay the fees which were justly demanded. Therefore, they had a large class of children whose parents would be glad to give them another year or two at school,a,nd to have another place to send them to.lVlany of the children were unable to get employment at once; they ran about the streets and got into mischief instead of learn- ing what would do them good. As educaitonists they should all be agreed that until some school accommoda- tion was provided for those children, there was a blot upon their system which they ought to endeavour to wipe out,so far he took it that they were agreed. Now came the question—What is the best kind of school? Two had been mentioned—the higher elementary and secondary school A. For some time he had found considerable difficulty in making up his mind. He had at first rather leaned to the higher school, but what he had learnt since, had shaken his convictionsvery considerably, and it was found that the curriculum in the secondary school need not be, as he had thought at one time,almost entirely scientific, but fairly general. The grant was considerably larger and the age limit higher, and these seemed to him to be distinct advantages. A year's delay would bea serious matter, and; it would be a great pity to put off a step that was BO necessary. They had buildings—they might not be perfect in every respect— and could get the additional staff necessary as a. temporary expedient. It would, therefore, be well to utilize the buildings. He appealed to the mover and seconder of the amendment to withdraw it. He would like to see a 6d. fee-; it was quite as much as the children-of working parents ought to be made to pay. (Hear. hear.) Councillor WILKINSON said this was no new thing. It had been argued for a couple of years, and he should vote for the resolution. He desired to see such a school opened forthwith.

'Alderman BENNETT said he had been inclined on the whole to support the amendment, but after listening to the discussion he was convinced that the balance of argument was in favour of the original proposal. Although the committee was small, some of ;the mem- bers gave the matter great and prolonged consideration. If they could re-consider the matter without postponing the establishment of the school until another winter, there might be some reason for the delay, but if it would be almost impossible to establish the school in September unless they took the preliminary proceed- ings, now they were bound to do so if they were to do justice to the educational requirements of the town. They ought to put the benefits of the school within the reach of the lowest and poorest child in the town if it had the necessary ability. The resolution did not com- mit them to anything in the matter of fees. He should, therefore, support. the original minutes. Councillor W. ROBERTS said that what had impressed him mgre than anything else was that there were 1,300 children qualified to enter a school of the kind proposed, and seeing that they were only proposing to provide for 100, it was an experiment that might very well be tried. What he lilted was that the admission would be competitive, and it would be a great encouragement to the young people to try to ,qualify themselves. He supported the suggestion that the fee should not be one shilling; what Manehester could do for 6d. surely War- rington could do. He also supported the resolution bemuse they had buildings for the purpose. Councillor W. PIERPOINT, in reply to Mr. Jolley said the Town Council threw out the resolution about, the extension of the institute, not because they had any objection to the foundation of a secondary school, but because the scheme was of an extravagant nature. In the circumstances they had no other course left. It was absurd to ay that he opposed the establishment of further educational facilities ; it was only a lot of gas and wind-bag oratory that might as well not have been uttered. (laughter) There was a point that seemed to him to reflect greatly on some of the educational institutions of Warrington. .It had been said there were numbers of boys and girls who had passed the sixth and seventh standards who were marking time. This was a great reflection on the schools that had allowed such a conditinh of things to exist, and alsa upon the Educa- tion Committee for not having taken earlier steps to bring about an alteration. Mr. PLINSTON said he would vote for the minutes, as it had been conclusively proved that what was proposed was a very necessary supplement to the voluntary educa- tion in Warrington to-day. Councillor CRAW said that with his seconder's approval he would withdraw the amendment. The dis- cusdon had done good and had enlightened many people. He should like to say to Mr. Jolley and others that he was not opposing the scheme ; he simply wishei to refer the minute back for further considgration. The Rev. E. J. Wthr.cocics expressed his willingness to withdraw.

Alderman MONKS, in reply, said he wished to con- tradict as strongly as possible the opinion that there had been any desire to rush this thing through. There were six persons presents at the meeting, and they all remained to the end.

The Rev. E. J. Wir.r.cocKs : Mr. Stevenson went before it was over.

Alderman MONKS : He waited until we had come to a unanimous decision.

The R3V. E. J. WILLCOCKS : He went before the voting.

Alderman MONKS : He expressed his hearty concur- rence in the proposal; and instead of being discussed at the fag end of the meeting, much more than half the meeting was occupied with this mrticular resolution. The only one who opposed was Mr. Willcocks, and he finally said he would not oppose any longer.

WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

MONTHLY MEETING.

Formation of a Secondary Day School

Sanctioned.

The adjourned June meeting of the • Warrington Education Committee was held on Monday evening at the Town Hall. Mr. F. Monks J.P., presided and there were also nresent :—The Mayor (Mr. J. C. Parr), the Revs. E. J. Willcocks, M. Stevenson, and N. Wilson, Aldermen Monks and Bennett, Councillors Brophy, Craik, Davies, Dodds, Upson, Ellison, James Evans, Hulse, Pierpoint, J. Roberts, W. Roberts, Rogers, Tinnion, and Wilkinson, Major J. D. Buckton, Messrs. W. Bibby, R. T. Fairclough, E. J. Guest, W. Madden, E. Plinston, J. Fulton Smith, George Walker, A. J. Jolley, T. May, and J. Moore Murray (director of education). icu COMMITTEES OR SUB-COMMITTEES?

The CHAIRMAN moved the adoption of the minutes of the last meeting, and. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY seconded.—Major Bucktoa observed that in the minutes the sub-committees were called committees, and therefore, he took it, the minutes were not correct.—The Chairman said it was decided by the Education Committee to call them committees.—Major Buckton contended that they had no authority to cal them committees.—Mr. T. May supported the protest. It seemed to him that committee was a committee of the Council. Then they also had committees, sub-com- mittees, and sub-sub-committees, and they were reduc- ing the thing to an absurd degree by dividing the work and making themselves non-entities unnecessarily.—The Chairman said he did not think it mattered very much. There was no difficulty arising from it.—rhe minutes were then agreed to.

SCHOOL FEES.

The DIRECTOR read the following letter he had re- ceived from the Rev. J. Davies :—" 5, Folly-lane, War- rington, June 10th. Dear Sir,—At a meeting of "The Association of Foundation Managers of the Church of England Day Schools in the County Borough of War- rington, held on Monday last, June Bth, I was in- structed, as secretary, to submit to you the resolution below as of urgent and great importance, and with the object of facilitating and making as efficient as possible the management of the various schools :—Apportion- meat of fees. What proportion of the school fees the Education Committee proposes-to pay to the managers in accordance with Section 14 of the Act, and when the first !instalment will be paid ?"—Very faithfully yours J S. DAVIES."

The CHAIRMAN suggested that the letter be referred to the Finance Committee to deal with,

Councillor WILKINSON seconded, andihe proposal was agreed to.

SILVER-STREET FOUNDATION MANAGERS.

The DIRECTOR OF EDUCATION stated that he had received an order from the Board of Education explain- ing how the foundation managers for Silver-street Wesleyan School were to be elected. Those managers who had already been elected would remain in office one year and then for the following year they would be appointed according to this scheme. Councillor UPSON said that when their managers were elected they did not know then that the parents and subscribers had the power to elect, but only to nominate. Had they known that they had the power of direct election the .trustees would have allowed the managers to be directly elected. The matter was referred to the General Purposes Committee on the motion of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Councillor TENNION. THE APPOINTMENT OF CHIEF CLERKAc

The CHAIRMAN moved that the question of appoint- ing the chief clerk and other officials be referred to the Finance Committee for consideration, and to make recommendations.

Mr. A. J. JOLLEY seconded, and the motion was agreed to.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S SUGGESTIONS.

The Town Clerk forwarded a resolution passed at the last meeting of the Health Committee, recommending the committee to give instructions to the headmasters of schools to notify alleged or suspected cases of infectious disease, and also that instruction be given to girls in the higher standards on the feeding of children. This was referred to the School Management Committee on the motion of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Coun- cillor DODDS.

Mr. MAY pointed out that children who were sent to school and happened to be late generally spent the hours that should be spent in school in running about the streets, and in order to do good to the children they might do them a lot of harm by committing them to the streets when they ought to be in school. RUGBY FOOTBALL.

NORTHERN UNION COUNTY FIXTURES.

On Saturday afternoon, at Huddersfield, the following fixtures for the ensuing season were arranged :—October 28, Lancashire v. Cheshire; November 14, Yorshire v. Lancashire ;November 25, Cheshire v. Railway Facilities. We are pleased to notice that the Great Central Railway Company have arranged, from July let, a new service of trains between St. Helens, Wigan and Warrington, thus avoiding the change of trains at Glazebrook as formerly. This arrangement will greatly expedite the journeys to Liverpool, South- port,' Sheffield, Hull, •,I\T ottingham, Leicester, London, cite.

Licensing. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Joseph E. Shaw was granted temporary authority to sell at the lied Lion Inn, Winwick-road, in lieu of the late Thomas Broomhall Malkin. Henry James Buckard was granted temporary authority to sell at the Hawthorn Inn, Orford-lane, in place of his late father. Walking Day. _ _ _

On Afonday- evening the Education Committee decided to close all schools in Warrington on Thurs- day and Friday next on account of Walking Day. This year the order of the church procession will be altered in order to prevent gaps being caused by schools' falling out of line to proceed to their respective parishes. The order will be :-1, the Parish Church; 2, St. Peter's; 3, St. Paul's; 4, St. Ann's; 5, Holy Trinity; 6, St. Barnabas ; 7, St. James'. The parish which goes furthest along the arranged route, there- fore, starts first, and the school which has to break off from the procession first leaves the park last.

SHOP-BREAKING AT NARRINGTON.

A stranger giving the name of George Wilson, was brought before the magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday on the charge of breaking and entering the shop of Mr. James Hallwood, &c., Wilderspool-road, between 9.30 and 11.55 on Monday night and stealing tilbs.of tobacco, a gross of matches, a box of lead pencils, a cash bag containing Is. 10d. a bunch of keys, and a bottle lime juce of the total value of 21s. The Chief Constable asked • for a remand for a week in order that prisoner's antecedents could be inquired into. It appeared that Mr. Hallwood left the premises secure with the exception of a back window about fifteen feet from the ground which was left open for ventilation purposes. At about midnight Police Constable Bennett was on duty in Wilson-Patten-street and met the prisoner carrying a small bundle. The officer concealed himself until the prisoner was close to him and then challenged him. Prisoner said the bundle contained only a pair of slippers but the officer made a scrutiny of it and found the tobacco. On searching the man he discovered the other articles. It must have been an awful disappointment to the prisoner to take a bottle which was so deceptive in appearance and find it to contain only lime juice. H 6 spilled this in disgust on the floor. It appeared that prisoner entered the premises by obtaining a scaffold pole, rearing it against the window, and swarming up it. He thus entered the back premises and gained admission to the shop by breaking away a zinc partition. He made an unsuccessful attempt to open the safe. On being taken to the police station he admitted committing the robbery in the way des- cribed. The name of Wilson was so often given by travelling light-fingered gentlemen that he (Mr. Talbot) suspected that it was not the prisoner's right name.— Prisoner was remanded for a week.

Memorial Service. On Sunday evening a memorial service to the late William Jesse Jenks, 30, Factory-yard, Warrington, the youth who was killed a few days ago in a cab col- lision in Chester New-road, was held at Holy Trinity Mission, Factory-yard. There was a large congrega- tion. The service was conducted and an impressive address given from Matthew xxv. 21 1;•37 Mr. H. Taylor P.C.M. Special hymns were sung, including the. deceased's favourite, "Fight the good fight." Miss Amy Place presided at the organ.
