===== 0002601_19030626_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

Naturally little Prinoe Edward of Wales greatly en- joyed himself at the children's garden party which was given at:Buckingham Palacelon Tuesday 23rd june,in cele- bration of his:ninth birthday.lt is usaalfamongst ordinary folks to give a birthday party upon such an occasion, but it is quite a new departure to have such a juvenile gathering at one of the Royal palaces. Their majesties however are always ready to show their kindness to little children, and it may be said that none have more indulgent granqparents than those of the Prince and Princess of Wales.

Dublin Castle , like Holyroed Palace, is scarcely fit for a Royal residence, and should the King verify rumour by establishing a permanent Royal residence in Ireland, the choice would be between two or three private estates, one of which would have to be purchase d by the country. In this respect Lismore Castle, th Duke of Devonshire's seat in Co. Waterford, is mentioned as being particularly suitable, as is also Muckross House Killarney, which belongs to Lord Ardilaun. It is however thought that Rockingham House in Ros- common, would have the best chance of being selected. The estate is one of the most extensive and beautiful

• in the country, situated about two or three miles from i i Boyle and it s hoped that the King and Queen will visit it, when their Majesties are in the West of

Ireland. The fact that Miss Hilda Hadson has secured at Cam- bridge a position equal to that of the Seventh Wrangler, serves to draw attention once again to the injustice Inflicted upon women by the Universities of Oxford and Cambridge, which refuse their degrees to women. In this respect these institutions stand almost alone among the great universities of the world, and almost alone among those in the United Kingdom. The first English University to concede this measure of justice was that of London, which obtained a new charter for tke pur- pose in 1878. Durham followed in 1895, and jeall the new British universities women are admissible to the same privileges as men. In Scotland the degrees in arts, science, and medicine have been opened to women. It is not altogether without interest to note that in the same papers which announced the success of Miss Hud- son, it was reported that at Oxford the fees. were being raised in consequence of the needs of funds, and that at Cambridge there was a shrinkage of college incomes. Women, at any rate, can scarcely be expected to bequeath money to Oxford and Cambridge while the present system is maintained, and it need scarcely be a matter for wonder if men who sympathise with women on this subject, should bestow their money elsewhere. The "Clearance sales" are already beginning,and they will this year present exceptional opportunities for making good purchases which visitors to Londonwould do well to take advantage of. In a good season the large emporiums readily dispose of their .stocks, and

wlren the "sales "come" on, alarge quantity)of the 'goods

offered are cheap lines esPecialli piovidel .for the pur- pose, and the "Greatly reduced " prices really represent their full value. But this year owing to the disastrous weather, the summer trade has greatly fallen off, and consequently the large establishments are left with quantities of genuine gods, which will have to be offered at cost price or less during 'July. There will thus be many opportunities for making profitable pur- chases such as Ewill delight the heart of the average . . .

bargain hunter. The drapers depend upon the weather almost as much as the farmers do for a good or badlseason. The latter have an old saying that a dripping June brings all into tune," but it may be doubted whether this sanctions such a midsummer winter as we have been having, and it may safely be said there is nothing so urgently needed by all classes as a fine July. The London shopkeepers still hope much from a spell of real summer weather, and also from the influx of French and American visitors, as well as those from the provinces. Prepara- tions are being enthusiastically and energetically pro- ceeded with in order that the French President may receive a cordial welcome on his visit to London, audit is anticipated that many private citizens will undertake the journey from Paris to London on this occasion. In these circumstances there is reason to hope that the President's visit following upon that of the King to Paris, may not be without abiding effect. One result of the Kin's visit to Paris was the large influx of visitors from Britain, and there will no doubt be many whp will spend their holidays in Franco as a consequence of the restoration of good feeling between the two nations. It is scarcely likely that the Parisian hotel proprietors and shopkeepers sympathised with the displays of hostility to Britain which appeared in French newspapers of the baser sort, for it is they who suffered the most from these outrages, and they must be very glad to learn from the practical evidence of their receipts that there is a resumption of friendly relations between the two peoples. Black and white was much in evidence at Ascot, and will.be quite the most fashionable combination for the remainder of the season. All • the newest clear white muslin gowns are touched with black, and the same may be said of all light colours, verging on white. Black too, is much worn. Black glace is being donned freely, also black voile, and sleek black cloth. Wraps,intended to be worn or dainty toilettes, are taking the form of three quarter sac coats, with big sleeves, fine cream blanketing being the fabric selected for the more dressy ones. These are touched about the collar and high oavaliqr cuffs with embroideries, which give the idea of soft old tapestry.

Strings appear on much of the headgear of the hour, and are usually of tulle or chiffon. To a quite young face they are quite becoming, affording to it a delightful framework, but the woman past her first youth should avoid strings of any kind, as in her case they distinctly push o'er the hand of time. Decided sweeps and curves are a feature of the newest hats and toques, and these are certainly a relief to the stiff outlines that have been so long with us. Moreover they are decidedly more be- coming to the majority of women. The deep swathed silk belt has quite taken the place of the narrow shaped band, so modish in the days of the pouch, aged the home dressmaker will find the task of building these deep belts a somewhat difficult one, as they need considerable skill, if the effect is to be allone could desire. It must be remembered too that it is not everyone who can wear a high belt to advantage. Such women should modify the idea, or remain faithful to the narrow belt dipping down in front, which is becom- ing t o almost everyone. Barley water is invariably associated with invalids, but as a matter of fact it makes an excellent beverage, for summer use. ft is refreshing, healthy, and sustain- ing.' First wash the barley thoroughly and throw the water away. The first water poured over and brought to a boil should also be thrown away. Then add to two ounces of pearl barley three pints of cold water bring it gently to the boil, let it simmer for two hours, strain, and add lemon juice and sugar to taste.

The Examiner, Friday June 26, 1903.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

Very many and varied are the different types of millinery one sees on every side, and indeed it is not saying too much that every woman can, if so she chooses, have her hats of her own original design, provided they are becoming for fashion. This season, exceed- ingly liberal minded, lays down no hard and fast rules in regard to what must or must not be worn. In one point, however, all hats are alike, namely, in regard to the flat plate-like crown, which, in some instances, is carried to such an extreme as to make one pause to wonder how the wearers of these exceedingly flat hats manage to keep them on their )heads at all, • for the loose strings, which conie from the back, are plainly designed for ornament and not for use. Most of the very flat- crowned hats are curved down over the face, sweeping upwards at the sides on to the back where there is again another depression over the hair, which is generally softened by a knot of lace or ribbon hanging down the back or a cluster of fruit or flowers. Among some of the most fashionable straws worthy of mention is glace chip, a straw .that is particularly light and wears well, which in burnt Tuscan makes

There is sure, in the usual order of things, to be a revolution in favour of high crOWns, for when once a fashion has be- come universally adopted it is a positive sign that it is already de mode for the votarie* of fashion soon weary of extremes of any kind. • The lovely hat eketched is of fine white chip, curving most beautifully from the front up to the left side and trimmed with sweeping ostrich feathers, soft draperies of fine lace, and some blush pink roses. It is one of the most perfect picture hats for summer wear one could imagine and is altogether worthy of admiration and imitation.

Picture hats are in great demand this season for wear with the exquisite muslin and dainty toilette of diaphanous materials. Some of these hats made of tucked ,chiffon and tulle are particularly pretty. These are to be seen in white or the palest shades of grey, blue, pink, or ivory, trimmed with just an aigrette or one long white ostrich feather held in place by a pearl cabuchon. Floral toques are also much worn, and are generally of the turban shape, worn well tilted over the face. Pale

A PRETTY BLOUSE:

Blouses are very much in evidence and are prettier than ever. Among the most popular is the so-called handkerchief blouse as shown in the illustration. One can buy these charm- ing spotted or flowered cambrics or muslins with a coloured border from about a shilling a yard and very easily make -up a blouse of this style at home, when a better fit is always en- sured. When blouses were first introduced little attention was given to the fit, but now-a- days it is a matter of supreme importance that the back and sides should lie as smoothly to the figure as possible without being tight while the fulness in front is carefully draped and does not fall in meaningless folds. For morning or country wear , there is nothing nicer than a simple blouse of this kind, and it is particu- larly becoming to very slender figures. There is quite a craze at present for embroidered

Some of the smartest gowns this year might be termed quite simple, for they'consist of very full skirts considerably gauged together with a full bodice drawn down under a deep folded belt, with a square or pointed yoke pf lace or embroidery and elbow sleeves finished by lace ruffles. Elbow sleeves are much worn finished gener- ally by a turned-back brocade gauntlet at the • elbow, from whence the . lace ruffle falls. MR. MOSELY AND HIS COMMISSIONERS.

[By Ma. H. Wllinum]. ' On at least two occasions the "Examiner "has editorally, drawn the attention of its readers to the patriotic and very laudable work of Mr. Alfred Mosely in arranging for and paying the expenses of a body of Commissioners representing workmens' organizations in five-sixths of Great Britain's in- dustries. I believe it is also true that Mr. Mosely, with the same liberal spirit, arranged to defray the expenses of a commission of experts whose object is to inquire into the educational systems of the United States. Mr. Mosely's attitude in this, proves that he fully understands the relationship that must exist between the school and the mill if success fs to be attained, and if England is to wake up to any purpose. I have just read Mr. Cox's address to his Warrington friends on " America's progress in the steel trade," • and I scarcely feel satisfied with it. I hope Mr. Alderman Monks's suggestion will be kept in mind, that the reports be reprinted, and I may add, that I be made the recipient of a copy. ' That the old countries have something to learn from the stalwart youngster is very evident. Ger- many thinks she had better wake up also, and now sends over to the States some thirty odd farmers who may possibly have the late Lord Derby's saying in mind, viz., "That he who makes two blades of grass grow where only one grew previously is a benefactor to his race." In reading over the speeches delivered at this interesting meeting of iron-workers, Mr. Alderman Monks's speech made me do a bit of looking backward He said: "What they had to do was to establish closer and more friendly relations between the work- men and the employers." I was pleased to read this because I see in the idea embodied in the sentence the only way to avoid the industrial difficulties into which we are all surely drifting. In looking back- ward I remembered reading a pamphlet written by Mr. :F.l Monks and published in 1901 in which the author says :—" If I am not greatly, mistaken in my observation of the methods now being adopted by the new Unionism to enforce the demands of labour, they are not only destructive of the true liberty and free- dom ;of action which has won for British labour its prominent position as the most highly paid in the world, but they will lead to combinations of capitalists in self defence and for mutual protection." In looking around for a way out of an Industrial difficulty I think it is always best to recognize, if you can, the influences that are at work in the minds of men of character and action. Here we have three men to deal with, the first of whom, Mr. Moseiy, thinks it wise to send over to the States a group of men whose object in coming ' to the States shall be to see and to learn for themselves the how and the why of the American Industrial suc- cesses the world over. True; he is also sending the pedagogue, and may, unless Mr. Chamberlain forestalls him with his" Inter-empire protection scheme " send a few Tory farmers over also, but why not send over a select number of financial magnates to find out the I reason of the shifting of the world's financial centre from:London to New York? Mr. Mosely's idea, be- hind the action, seems to be to enable the British workmen to see, in a few hours, how the American workmen can " rush "the job. Why not have taken the more economical path leading to Old Trafford and have introduced the commissioners to Mr. J. C. Stewart who has given an object lesson in showing how the" English labouring men can be trained to speed if he is managed properly." Mr. Steward claims to be an" adept" in "hustling" and "hustling" being a real genuine American product it shall have a little more notice further on. Now let us see where Mr. Alderman Monks stands in the argument. In the States the" Hustler" and the "Hustled," as a matter of fact, occupy the posi- tion of the proverbial lion and the , lamb, with the „result that the lamb soon 'becomes a vanishing I quantity. The worthy Alderman would alter all this I and I heartily wish him success in this altruistic idea but, and lam afraid this is a big " but," and ought to have a capital B, what will he do if the exigencies of competition compel him to" combine with his fellow capitalists in self-defence and for mutual protection" as Mr. F. Monks suggests? In all truth, 1 assert, and I know of what I speak, herein lies the whole secret of the matter. The English employer has, like his em- ployee grown up in,and been surrounded by conditions out of which he never dreamed of struggling. The competition has, until recent years,been with the man in the next street, now it is with he whole of the industrial world, which means t .ay the United States and Germany, and will 1, - aorrow mean the American Continent, Asia, and the ...fth quarter a the world. We all know what it me:,3 when a man starts out to attain an object. He taruggles, he hustles. When a score of men are after the same object, the struggle is keener ; and another word, competition, comes into play. The nation has now taken the place of the individual, and, by hook or crook, the Govern- ment must do something for the governed, or starva- tion clubs may increase in number, and that way lies social anarchy. If the strenuous life here outlined is the price we must pay for an existence, the sweetness and light of the life of which Matthew Arnold wrote are still in the dim and distant future. It may be that when Mr. Alderman Monks urged "more friendly relations between the workmen and the employees" that trite though misleading phrase that "the interest of the employer and the employees were alike," was in his mind. Now, really, what is the absolute truth of the position? Is it not true that the average workinan is after the most money for the least work in return, and the employer is wanting to get the most he can out of a men for the least he may pay as wages? If this is the outcome of the competi- tive system, what about the friendly reltions around the green baize of the mahogany? If any of my readers object to my presentation of the case, let them read the following quotation from a San Francisco paper "The contractors want to make all the money they can. When the unions forced them into recog- nition of certain hours of labour as constituting a day's work, THAT was looked upon as a dishonest practice. It was felt in the old days that a work- man should be only too glad to get out of bed at daybreak and work until dark. Now, even the stupidest and most selfish have come to recognize limited hours as a feature of American industry. And the enlightened glad:y admit that the well-paid, well-rested, independent worlier usually does more in, his eight or nine hours than he used to do in his twelve or fourteen. After the inauguration of the limited hour day the contractors invented what is known as a "rusher." The " rusher " is a young workman, in his prime and marvellously quick in his work as compared with the ordinary good, capable workman. On a job of bricklaying, carpentering or other work it was customary for the shrewd contractor to hire one or more rushers." Nominally t the "rusher" was paid regular union wages. But secretly the contractor paid him double wages, or even more than double wages. The " rusher " worked at high pressure hour after hour, day after day. The others could not possibly have kept up with him had he worked his fastest. But his instructions were to keep just a littleahead,that the others might struggle and do their best to keep even in their task, in order not to lose their work for apparent idleness. Thus the "rusher," a man of unusual skill, getting double *ages, went along well within his forces, while the, others were working themselves to death in order to keep up and not lose their jobs. The limitation of the day's output is based originally on the desire to squelch this "rusher" idea, or to put the quietus on the' very young and able workman, anxious to curry favor with his "boss" by making the pace too hot for the men working beside him. I thought of this quotation coming into my story a little later on when I deal with the state of things in. America but it seems to fit the "friendly relationship" point, aind says squarely to the Optimistic Altruist, No, We are not friendly, and our interests are not the same, therefore we prefer Mr. F. Monks's position, and accept his challenge. Employers combine to I protect your interests. Of course we cannot preveiff [ you, and do nothing to hinder your employees from I combining ta protect their interest. Now the fight is Imilaving tried my utmost to get behind the spoken , words I feel that some apology may be due and I as I gracefully as possible leave the person to deal further with principle. Mr. Alderman Monks and I have been intimately acquainted for a long number of years,weboth know something of Trade Unionism, and I am sure he

a hnd his will excuse the liberty I have taken with the v onoured name of Monks. I must now follow with arious deviations the fortunes of Mr. Mosely and his friends, as the serial story has it "to be continued." Guerriro-street, San Francisco.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church

On Sunday last Mr. Sage, from Widnes occupied thk pulpit in the morning, and Mr. Owens in the evening, when the choir rendered with excellent taste Mozart's Molett No 1, "0 God when Thou appearest," Mr. Spruce ably conducting and Mr. Fred Podmore supply ing the organ accompaniments.

===== 0002601_19030626_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Baptist Church, Golborne-street.

Mr. Edward Jones, of Warrington, conducted the morning service at this Church on Sunday. In the even- ing the preacher was Councilor James Evans, who based his remarks upon the words: "The Lord is my Shepherd, I shall not want."

===== 0002601_19030626_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Peter's Church.

On Sunday the choir sermons were preached at this church, the Rev. D. R. James, curate, occupying the pulpit in the morning. At the evening service theßev. G. B. Thurston, rector of Lymm, was the preacher and spoke upon the helpfulness of the choir in connection with the musical services of the church. It was, ha said, the duty of the choir, whose white surplices de- noted purity, to set an example to the congregation, and it was the duty of the latter to heartily join with them in singing to the praise and glory of God. The collection amounted to .24 Bs. Bd. Mr. P. Edgar pre- sided at the organ.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Woolstencroft—Hewitt.

The marriage was solemnized at the Parish church, on Thursday last week, of Mr. Charles Woolstencroft, Orford Street, son of the late Mr. C. P. Woolstencroft who carried on business as a bookbinder for many years in King-street and whose widow was recently licensee of the Griffin "hotel, and Miss Ada Hewitt, Willis street, daughter of the late Mr. William Hewitt. The bridegroom is employed in the clerical department at Messrs. Rylands Bros, works, and for some years the bride has been identified with St. Clement's mission, being a member of the choir and a Sunday school teacher. The service, which was fully choral, was conducted by the Rev. A. S.Cameron,and the choir from St. Clement's was in attendance, appropriate hymns being sung. A large congregation assembled to witness the ceremony. The bride who was given away by her brother-in-law, Mr. F. J. bride, wore a pretty dress of white muslin trimmed with silk, and a white chiffon hat trimmed with silk. She carried a beautiful bouquet of orchids and carnations, the gift of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids—Miss Rachfel Saundersand Miss Nellie Howard—were tastefully attired in cream delaine dresses 'with green spray, and large black picture hats. Their bouquets were composed of marguerites. Mr. James Monks, brother-in-law of the bride, discharged the duties of best man. Mr. Smith, the organist at St. Clement's Mission, presided at the organ and played appropriate selections including Mendelssohn's "Wedding March." As the wedding party left the church the bells rang out a merry peal. Later in the day Mr.fand Mrs. Woolstencroft departed for Blackpool where the honeymoon will be spent. They were the recipients of numerous presents, those to the bride including a handsome silver tea-pot from the choir and Sunday school teachers at St. Clement's Mission.

===== 0002601_19030626_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
Musical Successes.

At the recent examination held 15th and 16th in connection with the Victoria College of Music, London, Liverpool Centre, the following pupils of Misa Annie Hill, A V.C.M. London, 6, Napier-street, passed very successfully. Names and grades as follows:—Associate, Miss Jane Smith, Walton. 'This will entitle her to affix to her name the letters A.V.C.M., and also to wear the cap and gown of the College. Senior grade, Robert Bentley Bennion, Warrington; Intermediate, Pere?' Moores, Warrington; Junior, Mary Ann Hewitt; Primary, Lily Frith, Dutton; Joseph Bennion, Warrington. The Polyglot Society.

The members of this Society held their annual second excursion on Saturday. The weather was very favourable and about 20 members and friends travelled by cycle and train to Northwich. Thanks to the influence of Dr. Markel, the party was enabled to spend a thoroughly enjoyable afternoon in a visit to the Salt Union Company's extensive mine at Baron's 'Quay. Having safely accomplished the descent of 300 feet, the party made a tour of exploration, under the surveillance of the subterranean manager, who explained the various operations which were viewed. The mine, lighted by candles and variegated lamps, presented a truly pretty sight, and it was with a feel- ing of regret that the cars were re-entered and the ascent of the shafts made. After tea some of the large salt pans of the vicinity were inspected, many instructive and interesting details relative to the manufacture being supplied by Dr. Markel, who will at a prospective evening cycle run, give an address in German on salt and its formation. After an examina- tion of the Anderton lift,used for transferring shipping from the Bridgewater Canal to the river Weaver, the return journey was made via Marbury and Great Budworth. •

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must mall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday. - - - -

Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. -

IVe cannot under ordinary cir cumstancesundertake return thenuinuscripts which are not used.

Summer Holidays for Poor Children.

Sir,—With reference to the letter of Mr. Arthur 11. Lee in your issue of the 13th mat, I may as well frankly admit that I am cheerfully co-operating with that gentleman in the endeavour to bring about a success- ful result of his efforts to help poor children to get a summer holiday. I -am sure that it is unnecessary for sue to say anything to commend this object to your readers. I have suggested to Mr. Lee the desirability that we should at once take steps to organise (with a view to its permanent maintenance) a summer camp for the children, a Warrington camp, not dependent in any way on any other town or city for assistance. In the past this assistance has beenextended to Warrington children. But there are difficulties in the way, and we cannot depend on this assistance just at the time when it is most needed. So we must try to help ourselves. lam of opinion that there are enough kindly-hearted citizens in our own community, who, if appealed to, would furnish ample funds to effect what is desired. Mr. Lee falls in with my suggestion, andwe shall work with what we trust you will regard as a right aim: The establishment of our own summer camp. There is every reason for thinking that the Education Authority in Warrington sympathizes ,with, and will encourage the enterprise. In another column is an advertisement of a meeting to be held at the Town Hall on Tuesday next at 5 o'clock in the afternoon for the purpose of' formulating the machinery i for carrying out the purpose n view. In the meantime if there are any kindly 'disposed folks who are anxious to furnish the armoury with the neces- sary weapons of war, it itould strengthen the hands, and brighten the hearts of those who are warmly interested in the success of the venture, if they expressed their appreciation of the efforts being made, by at once for- warding cheques, bank notes, or cash in any form what- ever • however big, or however small the amount it will be thankfully acknowledged. There is no time to lost. Would those who wish to render practical assist- ance in the work kindlyattend the meeting ?—Yours,&c.' W. H. f. JENKINS.

===== 0002601_19030626_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
Music in the Park.

your last week's issue,l noticed a lettersigned "Onward," giving the Bands' Association advice, as to the class of music the bands should play in the park. Evidently" Onward," in the past, has not been n the habit of taking much interest in the programmes ren- dered, or lfe would have known that the class of music he asks for has often been played. The particular piece he mentions, the "Amen Chorus ", was played more than twenty years ago, and' the last time I heard it it was played by Fletcher's Band under Mr. Waterfleld, when they also gave a rendering of" Worthy is the Lamb." I may say that for a few years back, the bands have fought shy of the" Messiah," for nearly every church and ohapel in the town has worn it thread-bare, and grand as the oratorio is, one gets tired of repetition Again, many of the parts cannot be effectively rendered by a brass band. For instance, no combination of instru- ments, with the exception of strings, can give adequate effect to the " Pastoral Symphony." With regard to the Volunteer Band and their music, I can speak with authority, and it may be news to" Onward" that this band alone has played in the park upwards of twenty numbers from the Messiah," twelve from "Elijah," including "Oh Baal hear us," seven from "St. Paul," including that difficult chorus "0 great is the depth ;" selections from Beethovens' "Mass in C,";Haydn's Ist Mass; "Samson '-" "Creation" Mozarts 12th Mass; Kaliwooda's Mass ; "Judas Macca- bmus "; "Moses in Egypt ;"Weber's Mass in A; besides sacred solos, anthems, and selections from Sankey and lVfoody's hymns. I leave your readers to judge if that is not a fairly good selection for one band. "Onward" should remember the Bands' Association is trusted by the Parks Committee to cater for the general public,and not for individuals, and, from the programmes I have heard this year the bandmasters have done their work most admirably. I think it would be better if "Onward" and others, who have complaints to make, would lodge them with our indefatigable secretary, Mr. Wadsworth, or myself, and, if reasonable, depend upon it we will bring them before the committee. I have heard all the bands play this season,and have been much pleased with their general improvement and the splendid class of music they have played. What your correspondent means by "Bazaar Music," I am at a loss to know; if he meanssecular music, I cannot agree with his views, for to me, all music when well rendered is sacred. What could have been more pleasing or elevating than "Beloved Star "in "A Casket of Gems," as played by Crosfield's band last Sunday? I feel sure the great majority of the audience would get more pleasure from it, than from "We never will bow down," though personally, T thoroughly enjoyed the latter quite as well.

• In conclusion, allow me to hint that "Onward" might rather encourage than discourage the bands in their efforts. He should remember that it is largely a "labour of love" with them, for what,they receive from the Corporation does little but pay for the music for each programme, and many of the bands have other ex- penses e.g., rent of practice room, tuition Stc. Some have got up entertainments during the winter to raise funds, but the result has been most discouraging, for

instead of profit, the usual outcome was lO'ss of many pounds, and yet the critical public would flock in their thousands to be taken in by Yankee cuteness and .advertisement.—Yours &c., THOMAS ARMSTRONG. (Chairman, Bands' Association Committee).

What is a Bad Trade Unionist?

Sir,—Kindly allow us space to reply to the letter, and free advertisement (!) " signed " Edward Smith which appeared in your last week's issue. We have followed the example of other societies, and have made this ques- tion of an advance in price a Trade question; besides a Society question. When Trade Unionists agitate for an advance in wages, they always try to get the non- union men to go with them. If there application for a price is refused and they come out on strike, they bring out the non-union men and pay them strike pay out of the funds, the property of the Union. Any novice in Trade Union affairs knows this is a well established method of conducting a strike. Now it. was manifestly to our advantage to get all the hairdressers to act to- gether. Those who promised to act with us were sup- plied with our notices. Mr. Smith objects to this. Well ! if we are wrong all the Trade Unions are wiong also for there is no difference in principle between giving printed notices to non-members in our trade and other unions giving strike pay to non-unionists. We are prepared to let your Trade ilUnion readers judge. We wish also to ask of what avail is all Mr. Smith's boasting of his union secretarial work, when at the test- ing time he has failed us. There are others who have done good work for the Society,and who have not proved wrong.. _ _ _

We-give a few reasons why we consider Mr. Smith a bad Trade unionist. At the first meeting called on this question, he threatened to resign and "chuck out" of the Society if we persisted in the matter, this not- withstanding that he and another were the only two who opposed it. Trade Unions should be governed de- mocratically, the majority should rule. Yet here we have two who wanted to" boss the show " irrespective of the desires of their fellow members. Is this good Trade Unionism? Among the reasons he gave for his opposition were these" he couldn't get the increased price "—although by the way he is boasting now he is getting it. We pointed out that others in similar localities to his' were prepared to advance, and as a matter of fact they are getting the advanced price now. Then he said he was afraid of loss. We answered that we couldn't see how he could lose, but when we promised to guarantee him against loss he contemptu- ously refused. We asked him to conduct his business mhis own way, but make a minimum price of lid. But no! he said he would get it where he could. This is a principle that will break down any trade union, a man who wants to be on both sides of the fence. Every effort we made to bring him to a reasonable state of mind was treated with scornful contempt. Worse than this he carried his bitter opposition to an effort in at least one case to influence one of the members to break away. But he failed. And in this effort to break down the movement:he has made statements about some of our members in such a way as to cause very erroneous impressions. A canvass of the whole of the trade was made and wc.) had an overwhelming majority with its, and Mr. Smith knows it—notwithstanding his figures of attendance at our meetings. But this was of no avail with him,and we say he has bitterly and stupidly opposed us from the beginning. Now we will let your readers judge whether the com- ment made at the Trade. Council—that he was a bad Trade Unionist—ls TA true and justified. Let us say that Mr. Smith is not the only hairdresser in Warrington who knows how to con- duct his business properly, nor has he a monopoly of trade requisites. We are glad to say that our customers have received this advance very kindly and our thanks are due to all those who have helped us. We are also increasing our membership. We ask you to ex- cuse 'this trespass on your space. We have no intent!on of entering into a long correspondence with Mr. Smith, but we are prepared to put our case before the Trades Council if Mr. Smith will do the same.—We are yours Ike. THE COMMITTEE WARRINGTON HAIRDRESSERS' SOCIETY.

SUMMER HOLIDAYS FOR POOR CHILDREN. Proposed Seaside Camp. A commendable effort to provide a seaside camp for poor Warrington children during the summer holidays has just been set on foot. It is hardly necessary to point out that were it not for the kindly interest taken in them by sympathetic townspeople, there are many poor little dwellers in the slums who would never know what it was to enjoy the benefits of a holiday under the healthful and strengthening influences of the sea- side. For some years past it has been the custom to send about one hundred Warrington lads to the summer camp at Birkdale organized in connection with the Manchester and Salford Boys' and Girls' Refuges and Children's Aid Society,the necessary funds,of course 'being collected from Warrington friends of the move- ment. This arrangement will, however, be no longer practicable owing to the clashing of the Warrington and Manchester school holidays, and Mr. A. H. Lee and Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins who have taken a keen interest in the scheme so far as Warrington is concerned, decided to call a public meeting to consider the advisability of establishing a summer camp for Warrington boys and girls. In response to their appeal there was a good attendance at the meeting which was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening under the presidency of the Mayor (Mr. J. Charlton Parr). Amongst others present were the Mayoress, Mrs H. Roberts Mrs. James Fairclough, Mrs. Carleton, Mr. A. H. Lee, 'Mr. W. H. J.Jenkins, Mr. F.Monks, Alderman Monks, the Revs. W. Bracecamp, J. Davies, and B. Percival, Fathers Wilson and Whittle, Mr. J. Moore Murray, and Mr. H. Taylor. The MAYOR said they had met to promote the very useful movement for sending poor children to a summer camp. He' thought the object Of the institution was pretty welPknown in Warrington. Hitherto it had been supported through Manchester, and by contributing a subsidy of about £27 Warrington had been able to send 100 children to the camp. He was informed that Manchester could only provide for her own necessities and therefore the children they had hitherto been able to send from Warrington must either be sent by the Warrington people or else not go at all. He was sure it would be a great disappointment to the supporters of the movement if they were obliged to shut their doors and say they could not provide a week out for the children. Not only did the-annual week out benefit the children physically and morally, giving them fresh air and sending them to a place where discipline and cleanliness and other virtues were enforced, but it also gave thechildren ambition to leave their present surround- ings and taught them that there were pleasanter and happier places than the slums in which unfortunately they were born and bred. They obtained from theirholiday laudable ambition to see more of the world and to go into eparts pleasanter than their own surroundings. They saw the beauties of nature and felt all the charm and loveliness of open air, and he hoped that in direct- ing their eyes to the beauty of nature they might at the same time teach the children " to look through nature up to nature's God." (Applause.) Mr. W. H. J. JENKLNS read several letters regretting non-attendance. Miss Bessie Dutton contributed £1; the Rev. John Yonge expressed his willingness to help in every way he could: Mr. W. Long enclosed a cheque for £1 Is.; Mr. F. R. Roberts saidthe movement would receive his cordial support; Mrs. Broadbent thought the idea an excellent one, and would contribute to the fund; Mr. Henry Roberts wished the movement every success, and sent £l. is.; the Rev. M. Stevenson would do everything in his power to aid the object in view; Mr. McGregor was in sympathy with it, and would be glad to subscribe; Mrs. Long sent a cheque for £1 is. A telegram was also read from Mr. Long wishing the meeting every success. Mr. A. H. LEE said the launching of a new charitable organizationwas not to be done lightly. People said there were too many calls upon their generosity, and it was certainly only in consequence of a very pressing need that a step of that kind had been taken. Some told them that there was a danger of pauperizing the masses; and that in this work they were going very near I it. For some years he had been engaged in carrying on I the work, and the necessity for it had been impressed strongly upon his mind. He did not think there was a better means by which poor children could be kept free from the danger of pauperization than by sending them to the summer camps. The Chairman had put matters very clearly and concisely, and had summed up the general advantages which a summer camp would con- fer upon the children. Although, there might be some danger theoretically of pauperizing the parents and lessening their feeling of responsibility for their children, there were other ways of looking at the question. He felt strongly that they, as a Christian community, had enforced upon them duties which it was not for them to disregard. To give money to people was to impoverish them and rob them of their self- respect, but by sending children to the camp at the sea- side or in the country from which they would return in a more vigorous state of health, they were.really doing something for them which might go to the building up of a more vigorous ang healthy, life. He had referred toithe argument that they were taking responsibility from the parents. There was the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, which had for its object the enforcing of responsibility on the parents and it did so with marked effect. But the cortununity had a responsibility as well as the parents. The com- munity was responsible for the many temptations which were put in the way of parents and the question as to how far moral conditions were due to environment pro- vided food for serious reflection. During the last ten years a number of boys had been sent from Warrington to the Birkdale camp, which was organized by the Man- chester Boys' Refuge. Last year the number sent was unfortunately smaller than usual owing to the clashing of the school holidays in Manchester and Warrington. The same difficulty remained this year, but in order that the boys should not lose their holiday the School Attendance Committee kindly agreed to allow the boys to go to camp before the summer holidays commenced. They could not expect that such conditions would be allowed in future and it seamed to him that, the only reasonable step to be taken was to organize a camp of their own. Personally he had a feeling that the benefits of a camp onght to be open to boys and girls, but the Manchester camp was for boys only. Again in the Manchester camp there was a Protestant service, and some of their Catholic friends naturally had a strong I dislike to sending their children to a camp which was avowedly conducted on Protestant principles. By organizing a camp of their own they would be entirely free from such di,ficulties. The time at their disposal to organize a camp for this year was very limited, and he suggested that a camp already established at More- ton, between New Brighton and Hoylake, should be acquired for two weeks. It contained ten tents, which would accommodate from 100 to 120 children. There was also a playroom, cooking houses, &c. Mr. J. M. Murray was of opinion that at least 400 children ought to be sent, and he would, therefore, suggest that they should have ten additional tents. Altogether the cost of keeping 400 children at the camp for the two weeks would be about £l7l. £27 was the cost of sending the 100 children in previous years. Surely a town like Warrington should be willing and able to support such an organization, and he moved that a Warrington Sea- side Camp Committee he formed to provide summer holidays for poor children. Mr. F. MONKS asked what necessity there was for 400 children to be sent. He thought they would find it very difficult to raise £l7l in a fortnight. He asked what the cost would be if only the tents already fixed were occupied. ' Mr. LEE: £7O or £BO leas. Mr. MONKS had pleasure in seconding the resolution, and said the number would be a matter for the Com- mittee to arrange. The Rev. .1. DAVIES suggested that the word " sea- side " should be left out as they might send children into the country for a holiday. Mr. F. MONKS said it would take more time to arrange for children to go into the country than they had at their disposal this year. The M,tvort thought that that and all other details could be decided by the Committee. Alderman MONKS asked if Mr. Lee intended the camp to be a permanent institution. Mr. LEE: Decidedly. The CHAIRMAN said the camp at Moreton might only be occupied this year, but the movement would be permanent. The resolution was carried. Mr. JENKINS said he had had a conversation with Father Wilson, and it was intended that the camp should be entirely unsectarian. The Rev. W. BRACECAMP : Do I understand that there is to be no religious service at all? Father WiLsos explained that if there was a sufficient number of Catholics at the camp to warrant it, they would try to obtain the ministration of some priest for them. If that was impossible they would desire the Catholic children to be left entirely alone in regard to their religious exercises. Mr. MONKS thought the common sense of the com- mittee would settle such a matter. Mr. J. M. MURRAY suggested that all those present should form a committee, mid that a sub-committee should be selected from them to do the work. Alderman MONKS asked what method would be adopted in selecting the children. Mr. LEE said he was glad the question had been asked. A very great interest was taken in the work by the school attendance officers, and he was very much indebted to them for the amount of work they had done. In recent years they had really done all the work, and he had simply overlooked it. They knew the poorest children, and those who most needed a change. The children had assembled at the School Attendance Offices and a doctor had passed them as being free from infectious disease. He did not think that they could find anyone more capable of selecting than the school attendance officers, as they knew the most deserving cases. Mrs. CARLETON suggested that the camp might be used for the benefit of delicate children and Mr. Lee replied that this proposal was being kept in mind. The following committee was appointed :—Mr. Lee, Mr. Monks, Mr. H. Taylor, Mrs. Henry Roberts, Alderman Monks, Mr. J. W. Parker,Mr. J.H. Smethurst, Father Whittle, Mr. J. Moore Murray and Mr. Jenkins, with power to add to their number. The MAYOR was thanked for presiding, and in reply,he said he would be pleased to increase his subscription to £5. At a committee meeting held at the conclusion of the public meeting, Mr. Arthur H. Lee was appointed hon. treasurer and Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, hon. secretary. Communications should be addressed to them.

The Examiner, Friday June 26, 1903.

Mr. E. Lloyd Jones assured the meeting that the re- quisitionists had no personal feeling against anyone. They did not. wish to dictate but simply requested that meeting to consider that vital question. He contended that they ought to have a school which would accom- modate the children of every ratepayer in the district, and asked what they would do if they had to find room for the children who were attending Warrington schools. He did not think their friends who were interested in the present school would lose if a new school were pro- vided. He was not going to introduce that bogey of religious education, but in a provided school they could all agree and the children would all be taught the same thing. What was becoming of the children in that dis- trict who did not attend school? The Chairman: We are supposed to have all the children here. You are commenting upon the laxity of the present education authority and we have nothing to show that they have been lax. Mr. Willett: It is a reflection on the teachers. Mr. Jones: Children are kept from school because there isnot sufficient room to receive them. (Cries of "No" and" Yes.") - The Chairman: The Parochial Committee however, hada case brought before them in which that excuse has been given. Mr. England contended that when he was a member of the Parish Council they hadcomplaints from time to time of non-attendance of children at school—very serious complaints. Mr. Howard: And the reason? A Voice: There was no room. Mr. Pearson: I have acted as chairman of the School Attendance Committee from its inception and I can honestly say that I never remember hearing a complaint that there was no accommodation for the children. (Applause.) Mr. Whitley: I have been on the committee for some years and that excuse has never been made. Mr. Bouchier said he had never heard the excuse given. Mr. Rose: When Mr. England was on the Parish Council we had quite sufficient accommodation. Mr. Jones resumedhis speech and expressed the hope that the resolution would be carried. MR. BOUCHTER'S VIEWS. Mr. J. W. Bouchier, after expressing regret that there had been the slightest hint of any religious feeling in the matter, said he should support the resolution because he did not think an inquiry would do any harm. They must, however,approach figures something like correctly. They were told that they might have to provide three fourths of the £6,000. He had been working it out and it seemed to him that they would have to contribute £2OO per year for thirtyyears to redeem the interest and principal. They would therefore have to pay a 6d. rate in addition to whatever rate the County Council levied on the whole of the county. They might possibly have to pay a rate of 9d. or is. He quite agreed that the accommodation &c., ought to be on a better basis, but he could not help feeling that they were somewhat premature in approaching the question, because they evidently knew nothing at all about the actual accommodation of the school. He contended that the requisitionists ought to have placed themselves in a position to answer the questions which Mr. Jones had handed in. Mr. Jones said he had obtained replies to similar questions from Mr. Ward, the Director of Education, and it was only out of respect to the Chairman that he handed the questions in. Continuing, Mr. Bouchier said he had been informed by a school manager that the cubic area provided by the school was sufficient for 635 coildren, and that the average attendance was 625. When the Act came into force he took it that the Authority would soon find out whether there was sufficient accommodation, and if there was not more would be demanded. In answer to Mr. Bouchier, Mr. Rose said the number of children attending the school from Wilderspool and Warrington and the number who went from Stockton Heath to Warrington would about balance. A Voioe : What about the Walton children? Mr. Bouchier thought Walton was in a much worse position than Stockton Heath. There might have been a combined action on the part of both parishes to build one school to serve for both. The expense would then have been divided. MR. ROSE AND THE ACCOMMODATION. Mr. Rose observed that Mr. England said he thought the managers were to blame for not bringing the facts of inefficiency, the illnesses caused by the drains, &c., before the public. The managers in the past had sp9nt Government money, except what was raised by priAte subscription,—and that represented the 3d. rate—and were responsible to the Government. Up to now the Government had passed the schools as efficient, so that he did not think they could blame the managers. (Hear, hear.) He (Mr. Rose) could furnish the meeting with all the report up to date, which were quite satistactory (Applause.) While he did not wish to make an acrimoni- ous discussion, he felt that the managers and all con- nected with the schools had worked hard to keep the villagers from having a school rate and it was unfair to blame the managers who at times had had to spend £3OO or more in the interests of the school. (Hear, hear.) Warrington schools could not refuse to take Stockton Heath children, providing they had the room. As to the ilnesses lie thought their school would compare favourably with those of any other district, as the aver- age was only two per cent. They had accommodation for 638 scholars and the average attendance was 627. When the new authority came into force on July Ist they would increase the cubic space to be allowed each child from 90 to 100 ft., and also increase the area from eight square feet to ten square feet. Something had been said about the scholars not being sent to school because there was no room. According to the Education Department, there was room. in the school. Their attendance compared favourably with Warrington and the school attendance authority ought not to be blamed He was pleased to find so many interested in tbe future of the schools, and, personally no one would like a new school better than himself. (Applause.) MR. H. J. WESTBROOK PROPOSES AN

STOCKTON HEATH'S EDUCATIONAL NEEDS,

IS THE SCHOOL ACCOMMODATION INADEQUATE? Proposal to Approach the County Council Defeated.

--- Stockton Heath's educational needs were under dis- cussion on Tuesday evening when a parish meeting called in response to a requisition signed by 16 rate- payers and presented to the Chairman of the Parish Council (Mr. J. H. Clarke), was held in the National Schools to consider the question of the need for the erection of a new school within the district. That great interest is being taken in the question was evident from the large attendance, the room being almost filled. Mr. J.H. Clarke presided and was accompanied by Mr. E. Bradshaw (clerk of the Parish Council). The Chairman said the question was a very important one and it behoved all of them to have the welfare of their children at heart and to secure for them a sound elementary education under the best conditions possible within reasonable limits. (Applause.) . THE EXISTING SCHOOL. Mr. E. Lloyd-Jones opened the discussionand at the outset said he felt sure none of them wished te approach the question in any party or sectarian spirit; but from the point of citizenship both present and future. (Hear, hear.) In order to put the meeting in possession of knowledge which was absolutely essential he would submit the following questions to the Clerk (Mr. Bradshaw) or the Chairman: (1) When does the Stockton Heath non-provided school come under the Education Act? (2) Have the managers been ap- pointed? If so, who are they? (3) Has the school building been passed as efficient to meetthe requirements of the new Act? (4) What is the total number of scholars on the books and the average attendance? (5) Is the present staff sufficient to meet the requirements of the Act? , The Chairman: Mr. Bradshaw and myself are not the educational authority, and it is, therefore, utterly im- possible for me to answer these questions. I know nothing whatever about the management of the present schools, and Mr. Bradshaw is no doubt in the same position. Mr. Bradshaw: Absolutely ignorant. The Chairman said the first question was common knowledge. The school/would come under the Education Act,when it was adopted by the Cheshire County Council. When the managers had been appointed the full number would be six. Four managers would be appointed by those who had interest in the school, one by the local educational authority and one by the Parish Council of the parish. Up to now none had been appointed by the parish because there had not been an intimation from the local education authority to do so. They were ex- pecting the request shortly. He knew nothing about the next question, while with regard to the succeeding question he might say that he was neither the school- master nor the manager, and it was, therefore, im- possible for him to answer the question. He was in total ignorance of the last question. MR. E. ENGLAND MOVES A RESOLUTION. Mr. E. England said the onus of calling the meeting rested upon the requisitionists. The request was only signed as a matter of form. They could have had more signatures,but they had the required number to meet the case. Not only now but for some years past some interest had been taken in the question of education, and there was a growing opinion that there had been a lack of school accommodation in the district. He endorsed the remarks of Mr. Lloyd-Jones that they did not wish to discuss the matter from a sectarian point of view, bat purely from the citizen's standpoint,and above align the interests of the children. (Applause).Heunder- stood the Education Act would come into force in the county on July Ist, and the present i was a good time to discuss the question. He sympathised with the headmaster and the teachers, because he felt they were much handicapped. He also sympathised with the managers who must have had some hard work te do, and anxious moments in trying to conduct the school in an efficient manner. At the same time they were not with- out blame, for the reason that they ought to have brought before the people the true condition of matters in the village. It would be as well to speak plainly and as far as possible to keep toithe facts. There were only two matters to discuss, namely, the extension of the existing school or the building of a new one. He was prepared to demonstrate that a new school i would be n the interests of the ratepayers, parents and property owners and above all, of the children. (Applause.)The population in 1901 was 2,560,and it must now be at least 3,000. The increase was more than the normal rate, as they had a great influx of residents. There was something in the air which seemed to be attractive—(laughter)---and they knew, especially mem- bers of the Garden Society, that there was something in the virtue of the soil. (Renewed laughter) They also said that rates were lower in that neighbourhood. (Laughter.) The ratable value for the present half year was £10,315 Bs. A short time ago it was a little over £B,OOO, so that it was going up practically by leaps and bounds. He understood Oat the school accommoda- tion was 507, there were on the books 783, and the average attendance was 627. There were children who came from without their borders to the school, but over 100 children from the village went to Warrington. A certificated teacher was only allowed to teach 60 children in his class, but as a matter of fact some teachers must be teaching more than the proper number. THE CHAIRMAN AND .MR. SHERBURN.

The Chairman said he thought Mr. Sherbum's remarks in which he termed that room a prison were very muck out of place and uncalled for. It was not proper to attack the managers in the way he had done. Continuing, Mr. Clarke said the County Council already had the condi- tion, of affairs in Stockton Heath under observation. They had appointed a sub-committee to deal specially with tae district covered by the Runcorn Rural District Council. When the new authority got into working ,[ order the district would have every attention. He was not going to say that flatly ought to submit to the present accommodation for any great length of time. He felt the time was drawing precious near when some- thing would have to be done, but they would be a little bit out of place in approaching the County

• . Council before the new Act was in operation. At that school they took in children from Stockton Heath, Wilderspool, Warrington, Latchford Without. Grappenhall, Stretton and Walton. If they turned the children from other parishes ont, as they had power to do, they would have ample room for Stockton Heath children. They ought to be careful in approaching the County Council,lest they had to bear the full propor- tion of the cost of a new school. If they had a new school let all thespirisoef he Intl mentioned combine to pay the cost. He preferred to wait for the proposals of the County Council, and if the inhabitants were dis- satisfied with them they could have another meeting. If they found the County Council were not treating them properly they could go to the Board of Education.

THE COWS HMG THE MANGOLDS AT FRODSHAM. A Farmer Fined.

Before Mr. W. J. Stewart at the City of Liverpool Police court on Wednesday John Ravenscroft of Hall o'the Hey Farm Kingsley Frodsham, was summoned at the instance of Inspector Quinton that he on the 12th, of May, did sell to the prejudice of thp purchaser, milk which was not of the nature substance and quality de- manded. Mr. Trubshaw prosecuting solicitor prosecuted and the defendant was represented by Mr. Thomas S. Steel solicitor, Warrington. John Mc Ghee stated that defendant was under a contract to supply him with milk and on the day in question he attended at Lime street station for the milk. He there saw Inspector Quinton take the sample.—lnspector Quinton stated that he attended at Lime street Railway Station and there saw two( tank- ards of milk consigned by the defendant to Mr. McGhee. He took a sample and divided it, as required lby Act of Parliament, one part being sent to the Public Analyst and another to the defendant. He produced the certificate of analysis, which stated that in the opinion of the analysts the sample contained 9.74 per cent. of fat, 835 per cent. of other solids, making a total of 11•09 per cent. ; that it was deficient in cream, and also that it had been slightly watered.—ln answer to Mr. Steel, the Inspector admitted that he took a sample out of another tankard, but that was all right. He had not the analysts certificate with regard to the other sample, but no proceedings had been taken with regard to that.—Mr. Steel urged that no water had been added by the defendant or his servants —He was ahighly respectable farmer, a man nearly 70 years of age, had been a farmer nearly forty years and had never before been summOned. He had been in- the habit of supplying Homer's factory for years and his milk had always been of the highest standard. The milk- ing was done by the defendant and his two sons and they would state that no water was added but that the milk was taken directly from the refrigerator and then into the tankards. Mr. Steel pointed out that it was most difficult to lay down a standard of quality as to milk and that the Board of Agriculture had appreciated this difficulty. The defendant had always endeavoured to properly feed his cows and he was at a loss to under- stand why the milk on this particular occasion came below his usual standard. The only explanation he could give, was that his cows had got in amongst the mangolds and having eaten an inordinate quantity the quality of the milk had in consequence bean effected. —Mr. Steel urged that having regard to the whole of the circumstances, the explanation he had given and especially the fact that the other sample of the same milk had passed the analysts test, and also the fact that from the analysts certificate of this sample for which the defendant was summoned, showed that the quality was on the broad line of the standard laid down by the Board of Agriculture the case was not one for a severe penalty.—Mr. Stewart "Forty shillings and costs."

'Death and Funeral of Mr. T. C. Brooks. • The death occurred at his residence, 12, Be,wsey- street, yesterday week of Mr. Timothy Connelly Brooks, who had been in the employ of Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Ltd., for nearly forty years. The deceased, who was 57 years of age, had been ailing about two mmths, an internal complaint; being the cause of death. He is survived by a widow, three sons and a daughter. Mr. Brooks was very popular withl his fellow employees at the brewery, having worked his way upto the responsible position of head of the building department. He was prominently identified with the Conservative Club, and as vice-chairman of , the Town Hall Ward Committee his services at elec- tion times were always valued. He was a mem- ber of the Independent Order of Oddfellows, Minachester Unity, and held the position of vice-presi- dent of the Chirdren's Burial Society connected with it. —The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Monday. In addition to the relatives, Colonel W. H. Walker, M.P., was present, together with a large number of other representatives of Messrs.Walker,and'Son includ- ing Messrs. Jas. Monro, J. Fulton Smith, A. F. G Potter, W. James (chief out-door manager), E. Ellis. from Liverpool (secretary), S. Ross. T. ff. Clx and J. . Tilling. About 30 employees from the, deceased's own department preceded the cortege. There were also present :—Mr. W Holbrook, repre- senting the Conservative Association ; Mr. W Forsha.w and the following members of the in- dependent Order of Oddfellows: —Bros. A. G-. Webster, president; F. Taylor, T. Wadsworth, S. Taylor, E. C. Cooper J. Norburv, G. Ashton, W. Watson, F. Cl. Taylor:W. Taylor,G. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington once more tasted the sweets of victory on Saturday, and the victory was quite decisive enough to satisfy the most expect- ant supporters of the team. Earlestown were van- quished with very little trouble although Warrington lacked the services of Lynes, the professional, and W. H. Podmore. The Arpley enclosure was the scene of the engagement and there was a 'good attendance of spectators. ,Warrington had the first turn with the willow and for the loss of only three wickets compiled 167. It was then thought fit to apply the closure and Earlestown commenced their task. Only 88 runs were the outcome of their combined efforts, Warring- ten thus securing the victory by a substantial margin.

The feature of the match which must first be noted was the yeoman service rendered by J. W. Bibby, the Warrington captain. His batting reached a high standard and was responsible for 68 of the runs which fell to the home eleven. When the innings closed all ,efforts to bring about the dismissal of the captain had been unavailing and he was still occupying the wickets with B. Silcock who made an excellent partner his contribution being 45. Bibby hit eleven boundary strokes and Silcock five. E. A. Connor and P. B. Hawtin were'also in good form and were responsible for 28 and 14 respectively.i Alf Thewlis, the old pro- fessional, figured n thR home team, but was unable to add a single run.

Earlestown's most useful representative was S. Henderson who compiled 32 in capital style, J Miller and T. P. Howarth each knocked up 11.

E. A. Connor's bowling was most effective and accounted for 7 wickets at a cost of 33 runs. The Warringt= men fielded carefully and Duckworth made an excellent wicket keeper.

In the second elevens' encounter, the tables were reversed, Earlestown being triumphant by a 104 majority. The Warrington eleven were all dismissed for 112, and of this aggregate E: Pierpoiut knocked up 62 and D. Plinston 24. Plinston hit 19 boundary strokes. For Earlestown, W. Rylance wielded the willow in splendid style and was 89 (not out) when the innings closed. W. Hatton (33), H. Barr (31), F. Reade (27), and J. Burrell (15), were the other most useful contribut-Nrs. Harrison and Limond wrought much havoc with the ball.

There was an exciting match between Sankey Wire Mills and High Legit'', the Wire Mills running out : winners by one wicket and two runs. Gorton (47) and . Simpson (24) batted in grand form for High Legh, and Taylor (34' ) Woodcick (15) and Hatton (12) were the top scorers for the winning team. In the bowling department Surridge (High Legh) and N. and R. Cook (Wire Mills) gave fine displays.

Cycling.

The Lord Advocate of Scotland is a veteran cyclist but he is no judge of pace. In the course of a dis' cussion on the Scottish Licensing Bill this week he threw out a challenge which the most hardened road racer would decline tp emulate. Speaking as a cyclist, , he said he would cycle seven miles in less time than Mr. Hunter Craig, the Member for Govan, could walk a mile. Taking five miles an hour as the average pace a Member of the Legislature could endure on foot, Mr. Graham Murray binds himself to pedal seven miles on the road at the rate of thirty-five miles on hour!

===== 0002601_19030626_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
Angling.

The opening of the seasmn for angling for coarse 'fish witnessed' many local angletz venturing out in search of sport.. Many fishing localities were visited, as Chester, Rossett, -Stickle Trafford, the Shropshire Union Canal, the Bridgwater Canal and to say noth- ing of the river Bolliti. No great amount of sprt was recorded.

The river Bollin is now g3tting somewhat low and a good freshet is needed to enliven things up a bit. As a whole, the river did not fish quite so well as it has done on some previous week-ends, yet a fair amount of sport was registered. Mr. Ellis took a brace of trout by means of the black gnat and Mr. Mason had a nice mixed bag including a fair-sized trout. Mr. F. Bonnell hada decent mixed bag, which included a king-carp. Hr. J. Waywell recorded a specimen , gudgeon of 2ozs. On Tuesday last, Mr. Fred Bonnell was f irtunate in capturing a grayling, the first of the season from that river. Messrs. Brown, Bell, Allen and Webster have all recorded sport amongst the trout. The latter angler Was particularlyfortunate on Wednesday last in that he captured the heaviest trout 1 of the season. a beautiful fish of 19ins. long, 101 ins. in girth, and scaling 21bs. 7ozs. sdrs. The fish has been sent to Anstiss, of London, to be mounted.

The Mersey A.S. fished their opening match at Vale Royal last week-end with the following result :- Ist, H. Gatley, 11b. loz. 4drs.; 2nd, J. Graham, 13oz. 10drs.; 3rd, J. Ward, Bozs. ; and 4th, G. Spencer, Goz. Bdrs.

The Committee of the Latchford A.S. have decided to bold two evening contests for club members on Mon- days, July 13th and July 27th respectively in the waters •of the Bridgewater °anal at Grappenhall. There will be 'six prizes for eacll match. Messrs. A. Starkey, sec., H. Green and F. Y. Emson will act as a match committee.

The first contest hi connection with the Warring- ton Angling Association will be decided in the Bridge- water Canal at London 'Bridge, Stockton Heath, on Saturday, July 11th. This contest is open to mem- bers of the Association and to members of clubs affiliated to the Association for travelling facilities or for fishing privilege. The proceeds will be devoted to the benevolent fund of the society. All competitors will become members of that fund for the current year.

FACTS ABOUT FAINTING. I—Do you suddenly feel dizzy and faint? 2—Are you afraid to cross crowded streets? 3—Does it distress you to run upstairs? 4—Are you afraid to go to public meetings? s—Have you pains in the back? These are all symptoms of Mr. Richard Wilson, of Stamford Bridge, Yorkshire. They prove that he suffered from Kidney Disease. Giddiness, dizziness, and faintness are symptoms of kidney disorder. They are set up by the irregularity of the circulation; the blood being a stream of poison which the kidneys ought to take out of the system. The cure of such symptoms is pure blood, and pure blood is secured by making the kidneys well. One sure way of making the kidneys well is by using Dodd's Kidney Pills. Mr. Wilson writes, after trying them "I have very great pleasure in recommending Dodd's Kidney Pills, which are a grand remedy for giddiness, pains in the back, and all kidney troubles." Foot Rastag. C. J. Straw, the well-known and successful runner, has joined Sefton Harriers and took part in the two • miles' harriers race at Huddersfield on Saturday. The Sefton men were victorious, beating Salford and Farnworth.—At the Lancashire County Constabulary sports, at Fallosvfield, C. H. Longshaw, of Warring- ton, won the third prize in the 220 yards flat. H. Watkins, of Latchford, a member of the Warrington Rugby Football" A" team, did well at Wilmington Park, where he won the 100 yards sprint from 81 yards mark in 10 1-ssecs.,beating J. Moran, of Sankey Harriers, who started from the 71 pards marks, by a yard. Watkins also gained the first prize in the 220 yards flat from the 14 yards mark in 231 sec. In this race Moran came in third from the 101 yards mark. Swimming. At a meeting of the Northern Counties Amateur Swimming Association, held at the Mitre Hotel, Manchester, on Monday evening, it was decided to ask the Warrington Swimming Club to arrange for the use of the baths for trial matches on July 3rd, for the purpose of electing a team to meet the Southern Counties Amateur Swimming Association.
